She shut her stance. Only the stance, and kept the wall. Anwen saw her do it, and saw that it was hard for her, harder than anything she had done that morning in the lane. Her wall shook from end to end. Then she walked the far end round, a step, two steps, on one door, like anybody.

"It's loud," said Lyle, between her teeth. "One door. It's so loud."

"I know," said Anwen. "That's how you can hear it."

She went back down Tar Lane in the dark at half past seven. She had not told Lyle that a lodger could be killed, or how. She had not been asked. She thought about that all the way down to the harbour. She thought that she would tell Kiva what she had said, and that she would not tell Perrin, and she did not altogether know why.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-19.md

# Chapter 19 — Kiva: The Fitter with the Watch

Ruddock took Pike's dressings off at eight on Friday morning, in the Company's van yard behind the Long Quay, with the van's tailboard down for a table.

The Graywater van had come in on Thursday night by the coast road, three days from Meridian, with the rings whining and its slate-blue paint grey with dust. It stood now in a corner of the yard between two of the Saltmere Company's own vans, which were green, and longer, and had a kind of boat lashed upside down on the roof of each. Doran Mace's line had slept in the Company's barracks. Rhea Sorn had not slept anywhere that Kiva could discover. She was in the yard at eight, by the tailboard, with her hands in her pockets, watching.

Kiva had come because Pike had sent for her. He had sent a boy to Quiller's at seven with four words on a card. *Dressings off. Eight. P.*

"I wanted a second," he said. He was sitting on the tailboard with his arms out in front of him, palms up, on a folded towel. "There's nobody else in this yard has seen them go on."

Ruddock unwound the linen. He did it from the elbow down, slowly, a turn at a time, with his tongue between his teeth. The band at each wrist came last. Under it the skin was grey and new and shone, as skin shines on a burn that has healed too fast. The hands themselves were thin. They had been big hands once; Kiva had watched them tie three hundred bags. Now they lay on the towel as though they had been left there by somebody else.

"Close them," said Ruddock.

Pike closed them. The fingers came in, slowly, all ten. They came about halfway to the palm and stopped there, trembling a little, and stayed.

"Half," said Ruddock. "Last week it was a quarter."

"I can count," said Pike.

"Open."

The fingers went out again. They went out more easily than they had come in. Pike looked at them for some time.

"A cord's three turns," he said. "And a thumb to pin. And the little finger across." He did not say it to anybody in particular. "I'd want them all the way to the palm for the first, and the thumb hard for the second. I've neither."

"You'll have the first in a month," said Ruddock. "I'd not promise the thumb."

"I never asked you to promise it." Pike lifted his eyes to Kiva. "You've a hand up to here, I'm told." He touched his own forearm, just below the elbow, with one grey fingertip. "Your healer's been telling the whole quay."

"Mid-forearm, at seven this morning," said Kiva. "Aske says it'll be the wrist by four."

"And yours'll come back." Pike looked down at his own. "Well. We're a pair."

He nearly smiled. It did not come all the way, but it was there.

Rhea had come across the yard. She stood beside the tailboard and looked at Pike's hands, and then at Kiva.

"There's a Board warrant," she said. "We came south on it. The Company's stretched to the last man with the springs coming on, and four tears on the Longshore this week, and the Board's sent for vans from inland to stand on Company ground under Company eyes." She took a slate out of her pocket and did not look at it. "I've two nights left on your card. If the Company calls us on Monday, it'll be one of them. Pike rides as a voice. They'll lend us a catcher. You're Pike's second, as before."

"If T7 doesn't want me."

"If T7 doesn't want you." Rhea put the slate away. "That's the card. I wrote it."

---

Aske was right. At four on Friday afternoon the hand was cold to the wrist and no farther, and Kiva went out of the loft by herself.

She had decided at noon, sitting on the edge of her bed with her book on her knee. She had read Hart's second card again, which she kept between the last page and the cover. *What do the two fingers tell you through iron? Try it properly, and write down how it comes out, whichever way.* She had tried it properly. She had tried it on her aunt's surface plate and in her aunt's vice, on a free Saturday in the twelfth month, forty-nine times, and written down every one. Six in seven. She had never written back to him with the answer. She had meant to. It had seemed better said than written.

She did not tell Orla. Orla had a stick and a bad knee and a habit of coming. She did not tell Perrin, because Perrin would have wanted to know why, and she was not certain why. She took her coat and went down Quay Street and along the Long Quay toward the head of the basin.

The low town began where the quay ended.

She had seen it from the boat, a wide flat country of roofs below the hill. She had not understood what it meant until she walked into it. The street went down a ramp from the quay, and then it was level. The houses on either side were small and close and neat, with their doors painted, and their steps whitened, and boxes of something green in the windows. And behind the houses on the right, where the harbour was, there was a wall.

It was taller than the houses. It was grey stone, with a parapet along its top, and from down here she could not see over it. She could see the masts of ships going past above it, as though the ships were sailing along the roofs. At high water the sea on the other side of that wall would be above the chimneys.

There were drains in the street. They were iron gratings every twenty paces, and from every grating came the sound of water running away underground. And under the sound of the water there was the other sound, the town's count, much louder here. She could feel it in her boots without kneeling. Four and four. The gratings rattled very slightly on it.

A child was sitting on a doorstep with a slate, drawing soldiers. Kiva knew what they were before she was near enough to see.

---

The pump house stood where the low town came up against the head of the basin.

It was the long stone building with the organ-pipe chimneys. From here it was enormous. It stood on a bank of masonry with the brown water of the basin on its seaward side and the low town's roofs on the other, and it hummed. The door was open. There was a brass plate beside it, green with salt. *SALTMERE HARBOUR PUMP HOUSE. BUILT 2398. RE-CELLED 2611.*

Kiva went in.

It was a hall. It was taller than the Ferren pump house and three times as long, and the light came down from windows high in its roof onto two rows of cells, eighteen on each side, along a gallery at the height of a first floor. The cells were the old kind, iron drums the size of barrels, each with a hood and a gauge and a brass handle. Below the gallery, on the floor of the hall, were the pumps. There were six of them, great black iron things like the legs of a beetle, going up and down. They went up and down together. Every one of them, on the same stroke.

It was very beautiful. That was the thing that Kiva had not expected. Six great pumps and thirty-six cells, keeping time with each other so perfectly that it looked like one machine breathing.

There was a desk under the gallery stair, at the end of the hall, with a green-shaded lamp on it and a calf book open, and a small woman in spectacles writing in it. And at the desk, with the tide table spread across half of it and a pencil in her left hand, sat Mara.

Kiva stopped.

Her aunt looked up. She did not drop the pencil. She did not stand. She looked at Kiva across the length of the pump house, past six iron pumps going up and down, and her face did something that Kiva had seen it do perhaps twice in her life. It went still in a way that was not calm at all.

"Ah," said Mara.

---

They went out onto the outfall gallery, because there was nowhere else in the pump house that was not full of the noise of the pumps.

It was a stone walk along the seaward side of the building, with an iron rail, above the basin. Below it, the pumps' outfalls came out of the masonry in six great iron mouths, side by side, and poured the low town's water into the harbour. The water came out on the count. It came in four great gulps, and stopped, and came four more. The brown water of the basin went away from the outfalls in rings.

"I came on Wednesday," said Mara. "By the Works' transit. With Rusk. I wrote to the officer here in the second month, the night I wrote to the Order."

"The night of the twenty-third."

"Yes."

"You didn't say."

"No." Mara had her hands on the rail. The left one, the reading one, lay flat on the iron; the right one held it. "I meant to tell you on the cart. Then I meant to tell you at the house of the Even Hand. Then you had a fever on the ferry and I was on a transit two days behind you, and I thought, I'll write it to Saltmere. And then I thought, she'll have had a week of standing in a strange town on one door, and here's a letter to say her aunt's coming to read the bank that everybody's proud of." She did not look at Kiva. "I decided it at the kitchen table in the middle of the night, and I didn't tell anybody until it was too late for them to argue. I've done that all winter. I don't like it in myself. I've not stopped."

"You don't have to read it," said Kiva.

"I said I wouldn't promise."

"I know what you said. You said it to me in the autumn, at Ferren, with your hand white to the wrist." Kiva found her voice was not quite level and made it so. "Thirty-six cells. You did four-and-twenty, and at the eighteenth you stopped feeling the iron. You've one hand."

"I've one hand," said Mara. "I know how many I've got."

Kiva stopped.

She had been about to say something more. She had been about to say *don't,* or *you mustn't,* or *if it goes as the right one went.* She had all of it ready, the way she had the parts of a crossing ready, in order.

She looked at her aunt's left hand, flat on the iron rail, reading the grain of it out of habit. She thought of a kitchen table, and a woman reading a card and laying that same hand flat on it. *You'll want my letter with it.*

It was not hers. It had never been hers. Her aunt had sat across a table all winter and watched her make a choice about her own hand, and had said what she thought, once, and then had not said it again. She had not owned it. She had let it be Kiva's.

"It's yours," said Kiva.

Mara turned her head.

"It's yours. Whether you read it or not. I'll not say anything else about it." Kiva put her own left hand into her coat. It was cold to the wrist. "I'd like you to know that I minded. That's all."

Her aunt looked at her for a long moment.

"That's the most grown-up thing anybody's said to me since the autumn," she said. "I find that I don't care for it at all." She took her hand off the rail. "Come in. There's somebody who'd like to meet you. He's been on the floor since one, and he's pretended not to know you were here for a quarter of an hour, which is the most tact I've seen in this town."

---

He was on the pump floor, between the second pump and the third, with a can of oil and a long-necked oiler in his left hand.

He was tall. That was the first thing. He was taller than Perrin and much thinner, and he stood with his shoulders a little forward, as though he had spent twenty years under the gallery and had got used to keeping his head down. He wore a fitter's blue jacket gone pale at the seams. There was a watch on a leather strap round his left wrist, on the inside, where he could see it without turning his arm. His hair was black and cut short, and it had gone grey over the ears.

His right hand was inside the front of his jacket, between the second button and the third.

Kiva saw that and felt something go through her. It was where she kept hers.

He put the oiler down on the pump's base and came across the floor to the stair. He did not hurry. He came to the foot of the stair and looked up at her, and she saw his face properly for the first time. It was long and brown and lined round the eyes, and the eyes were brown and set deep. It was a face that listened.

"You're the one who writes everything down," he said.

His voice was low and slow. The coast had got into it; it said its words in a long flat way, as Carrow's did. But under the coast there was something else, something she knew, and it took her a moment to find it. It was Meridian. It was the way the Old Yard said *floor,* with the *r* almost gone.

"Yes," said Kiva. "Rowan."

"Hart." He did not offer his hand. He did not have one to offer; the left was black with oil and the right was in his coat. "You came."

"You asked a question. On a card. I've the answer." She came down the last two steps. "I thought I'd say it, not write it."

"Then say it."

"Forty-nine times, on a surface plate and in a vice, on a Saturday in the twelfth month. Through iron, at a light touch, the two fingers say a thing keeps time. Six in seven. They never say what time. If I lean, they say my own pulse. If I'm frightened, the same." She heard herself saying it as she wrote it, in order, the way she had laid it out on the Works paper. "It's a reason to look. It isn't a reading."

He looked at his watch.

It was a small thing. He turned his left wrist an inch and glanced down at the inside of it, as a man glances at a watch before he answers a question. Then he looked back at her.

"Six in seven," he said. "That's a good instrument."

"It's two dead fingers."

"It's a barometer." He took the oiler up again. "A barometer doesn't know what weather is. It knows that the air's pushing, and how hard. That's all it knows. You could hold it in your hand for a year and it would never once tell you *rain.*" He went back to the second pump. She followed him, because he seemed to expect it. "But if you look at it at eight every morning, and write down where the needle is, and look out of the window, and write down whether it's raining, then after a year the book knows what weather is. The needle never does. The book does."

He bent and put the long neck of the oiler into a hole in the pump's casing, and squeezed, once, and drew it out. The pump went up and came down. Four, and four.

"You asked what they tell you," he said. "That's the wrong question. That's the one I asked for ten years." He straightened. "Ask them the same question at the same hour, every day. Don't ask what. Ask yes or no. Write it down. Never ask on purpose, when you want an answer. Ask when it's time to ask." He looked at her. "It'll never tell you what time a thing keeps. Your book will."

Kiva stood on the pump-house floor with her cold hand inside her coat.

She had spent a year trying to make the two fingers tell her something. She had tested them and measured them and written down six in seven and called it a reason to look. She had never once thought of them as a thing that could be read like a gauge. She had thought of them as a loss with a small amount left over.

"How long?" she said.

"For mine? Three years before the book knew anything. Eight before I trusted it." He took his right hand out of his jacket.

---

It was a whole hand. She had not known what she expected, and that was the first surprise. It had five fingers and a thumb, and the shape of it was a hand's shape. But the skin was wrong. It was grey-white, like the wax of a candle, and smooth in a way that skin is not, without lines at the knuckles. The nails were ridged and yellowish. And the fingers lay curled toward the palm, not tightly, as a sleeping child's fingers lie, and they did not straighten. He did not try to straighten them.

"The Bench," he said. "At the Regional, twenty-one years ago, in front of four thousand people. My doors fought on a live cell, and the cell ran away, and the backwash came up this arm before the quench took." He said it as a man reads out a fault. "Your healer will have a word for what it did."

"Turned out," said Kiva. "He says he can ease a hand that's starved. He can't ease one that's turned out."

"He's right. I had ash put on it for two years, by people who meant well. It did nothing." He looked at the hand without much interest. "It doesn't feel. Not hot, not cold, not a pin. It doesn't hold. I write with the other, and badly; you've seen. But it aches."

"When?"

"When something near it keeps time." He held it out a little way, toward the pump. "It aches now. It's ached for six months, every hour of every day, since twenty past two on the seventeenth of the ninth month." His voice did not change at all. "Before that it ached at the pumps and nowhere else. Now it aches everywhere in this town, because the whole harbour's keeping time."

Kiva's left hand had come out of her coat without her telling it to.

"Mine said *keeps time* on the quay," she said. "On the first night. Through a bollard."

"It would. It'd say it through anything here." He put the hand back inside his jacket. "But there's twenty minutes a tide when it stops."

She looked at him.

"Come," he said.

---

He took her out onto the outfall gallery, past Mara, who had gone back to the desk with the small woman in spectacles and did not look up.

On the gallery, at its north end, there was a little iron landing, and from it a ladder went down the masonry to a grating at the water's edge. It was a heavy iron grating, set in the stone, over a culvert that ran under the pump house. Beside it, bolted to the masonry, there was a tall white board painted with black lines and numbers. *0* was at the bottom, at the mud, and the numbers went up in feet to *24* at the top, above the gallery's rail.

"The tide staff," said Hart. "The basin's. Not the sea gate's. They're different." He took a book out of his jacket pocket with his left hand. It was a small thick book with a cloth cover, very worn. "The table they print in the town is for the sea gate. Slack at the sea gate comes three-and-twenty minutes before slack at this grating, because the water has to come in under the gate and fill the basin before it stops. Your aunt found that on Wednesday night. She'd been reading my six minutes against the wrong table all winter."

"She said she couldn't make them agree."

"Nobody could. I couldn't, for a year." He opened the book and held it so that she could see. "I gave up on the table. I used the hand."

The pages were ruled in four columns, in his round childish hand. *Date. Slack, by the watch. Hand. Staff.* There were two lines for every day, one for each high water and one for each low, going back and back. In the third column every entry was one of two words. *Ache. Quiet. Ache. Quiet.*

"Every slack, I come down that ladder and lay the bad hand on this grating," he said. "With the pumps running. Four and four. It aches. Then the tide stops, and for a little while it's quiet. Then the tide turns and it aches again. I write down the minute it goes quiet and the minute it comes back." He turned a page. "Twenty minutes, near enough, at the springs. Longer at the neaps. Never the same twice. Never what the table says."

"But the pumps are running."

"The pumps are always running. They're keeping the low town dry." He shut the book. "I don't know why it's quiet. I don't know what the hand's listening to that stops. I only know that it does, and that I can tell the Works the minute it stops and the minute it starts. Nobody else in this town can, to the minute. Not with a table. Not with a gauge." He put the book back in his pocket. "So your aunt came. She's the first who asked."

"The cells that held," said Kiva slowly. "On the twenty-first of the eleventh month. The forty-one minutes."

"They were filled at twenty to four. The basin's slack began at three thirty-eight." He did not look at his watch this time. He did not need to. "They held while it was quiet. The one that held longest was filled the closest to the start. They went in when the hand began to ache again." He was quiet for a moment. "I wrote that down for your aunt. I didn't write why. I don't know why."

Kiva looked at the grating, and at the brown water going away from the outfalls in rings.

She had a question. She had had it since the autumn, since the night at Ferren with a keeper's book under a desk lamp, and a line in brown ink that she had read twice. *Somebody at Saltmere had a temperature as well.* This man had known what the bank was before anybody told him. He had known it with the bad hand. He had sat in this pump house at twenty past two on the seventeenth of the ninth month, and she wanted to know, very badly, whether he had been hot that night, and whether he had slept, and what he had seen if he had.

It was there behind her teeth. It would have taken six words.

She did not ask it.

If she asked, and he said yes, then it was hers. It would have come home to her by boat, and it would be worth half. She had decided that in the autumn at a kitchen table, with her hand on a pencil. She had thought then that it was a great deal to give up. She found now, standing a yard from him on an iron landing, that it was a great deal more.

"Are you all right?" said Hart.

"Yes."

He looked at her as he had looked at her from the foot of the stair, with that long listening face.

"You've a shut door," he said. "You told me so on the card. One line left out on purpose." He turned his watch on his wrist, an inch, and back. "I've never asked what was behind it. I've got some of my own."

"I know you have."

"Then we'll do very well," said Hart. He turned to the ladder. "Come at the springs, if you want to see the hand go quiet. Tuesday and Wednesday, at about six in the morning. There's a thing at the outfall at the springs that you'll want to see as well." He set his foot on the first rung. "I'll not tell you what. I'd sooner you saw it first and wrote it down."

He went up the ladder one-handed, with his right hand in his jacket. At the top, on the gallery, he stopped and looked down at her.

"Your sponsor's in town," he said.

"Yes."

"I thought she might be." He did not say anything else. He went in.

---

The second seeding lane went at two on Saturday afternoon, in the Ropewalk, against the Ropewalk School's second four.

They were younger than the first four, and they knew it, and they had watched Seven put Lyle on her knees on Wednesday. Kiva saw that in the first minute. Their catcher was a tall fair boy who called *run* and *lay* in a loud clear voice, as the book said, and their Hold and their blade were both laid. When Seven went wide along the north wall at the bell, the Hold did not run. She stood in the middle of the pen, laid, with her wall across, and let them go past.

"*Over,*" said Perrin, at the hurdle.

They went over. Anwen took the poles from under, and Jude, and it was three to Seven before the glass was half run.

"That's the wall that doesn't walk," said Perrin, coming back. "She saw what happened on Wednesday and she's not running. So she's no use to them at all where she stands."

The second four changed in the pause. Kiva watched their catcher talk to their captain, a stocky boy with a blade, very fast, with his hands. When the bell went for the second carry, their Hold did not lay. She came into the pen with her wall up and her stance shut, on one door, and walked it.

She walked it badly. She walked it from the near end, the way everybody did before somebody taught them otherwise, and the far end swung about like a gate in the wind. But she walked it, and it was in Seven's way, and Seven had to go round. Their blade came after Jude on one door too, unlaid, quick and loud. It touched Perrin on the poles.

"*Down.*"

Then their carry and their catcher took the Commodore and went for their hurdle, and Anwen's wall was a pace too slow, and they went over. Three. Three and a down. Four to three, to them.

"They've stopped laying," said Jude.

"They've stopped laying because we made it walk." Kiva was watching their Hold, who stood behind her hurdle with her forearms shaking, as Anwen's had shaken on Wednesday. "They're not as good on one door as we are. Nobody here is. They've never had to be."

"Then we stay on one," said Perrin. "We're better at it than they are. We've done nothing else since we were thirteen."

In the third carry, Seven was better at it. Anwen walked her wall from the far end, as Mace had taught her, and it swung round across the pen in a quarter circle and stopped where she wanted it, and the second four's Hold walked hers from the near end and it did not. Jude stood between their blade and the pass and lit, and touched him as he came. Perrin tapped once, and let his shoulders go, and Anwen took the poles. The Commodore did not go over; their catcher was at the hazel and would not be shifted. But he lay in Seven's half when the glass ran, and there had been a down.

"*Time,*" said the marshal. "A point to Seven, and a down. Six to Seven. Four to the Ropewalk."

There was a little sound from the rails. It was not the warm sound. It was quieter and more puzzled, like a crowd that has watched a familiar dog do a trick it did not know.

Perrin did not say anything at the hurdle. He went across the pen to the second four's captain and shook his hand, and said something that Kiva could not hear. The stocky boy laughed, rather ruefully, and said something back.

"What did he say?" said Kiva, when Perrin came back.

"He said they'd never walked a wall in a lane in their lives." Perrin was very red. "He said, *We don't have to, here. Nobody makes us.* I told him I was sorry for it. He said not to be. He said he'd learned more in that glass than in a month."

---

There was a woman at the rail in Saltmere green with gold on her collar.

Kiva had never met her. She knew the gold on the collar, and she knew the name, because it had been on a letter under the brass weight on her aunt's dresser. The woman was tall and grey-haired and brisk, and she wrote in a notebook with a pencil while she watched, without looking down at it. She was not looking at Kiva. She was looking at Jude.

He had gone out of the pen by the side door, to the steps that went down from the Ropewalk to the lane. He was sitting on the top step with his left arm back in its linen and his face toward the harbour, which lay below the whole length of the ridge, brown and flat inside the Mole, with the grey sea beyond.

The woman went out after him. Kiva went to the door. She did not go out. She stood in it, where she could hear, and nobody seemed to mind.

"Talley," said the woman.

Jude looked up. He did not get up. "Ma'am."

"Yeo. Saltmere Company." She did not sit down. She stood on the step above him with her notebook shut. "You stood between their blade and the pass in the third carry. You did it before he moved. What did you see?"

"His feet," said Jude. "His weight came off the back one. I've been watching people's feet since the second month." He looked at the harbour again. "A friend of mine taught me. He's on another four now."

"Renn."

"Yes."

Yeo wrote something without looking down.

"You've been looking at the sea," she said.

"I've been looking at it since Sunday week. From the boat, mostly." Jude scratched his jaw with his good hand. "I'd wanted to see it since I was small. I'd want it said that it isn't what I thought. I thought it would be still, mostly, and then waves. It's never still."

"No," said Yeo. "It isn't."

"Ma'am. What's it for, here? The ash."

Yeo looked at him.

"I've asked that of everybody," said Jude. "Since the autumn. A Marshal, and a foreman, and my own four. At home it's a hand, if it's quiet, and a floor, and a cell, and the Works. What's it for on the coast?"

Yeo was quiet for a moment. Kiva saw her look down the hill, past the high town and the low town, to the long stone pump house at the head of the basin with its chimneys shivering.

"Keeping the sea out," she said. "That's what it's for. All of it, in the end. Every dram the Company tins goes to a cell somewhere that keeps a pump going that keeps somebody's kitchen dry." She put her pencil in her notebook. "You'll hear people say it's for the Company, or the Board, or the Measure. It's for that. A kitchen in the low town, at the bottom of a spring tide, with the sea on the other side of the wall above the chimney."

"Then it's always the same thing."

"It's always the same thing." Yeo turned to go back in. "That's why I've asked the Works twice for its reader. If the thing that keeps the kitchen dry is a hazard, I'd want to know before the sea does." She paused on the step. "You stood well, Talley. On that leg."

She went in past Kiva, and nodded to her without stopping, and went back to the rail.

Jude sat on the step. After a while he said, without turning his head, "You were listening."

"Yes."

"I'd want you to have heard it." He looked at the harbour. "*It's always the same thing.* I've been asking since the autumn. Nobody's said that."

---

On Sunday at seven, in the loft, by the north window, Aske held the hand for two minutes by his watch and said, "Warm."

He wrote it standing, against the window frame. *Warm at seven on Sunday the 6th. A sulk of three nights. Five of six.*

He did not say anything else. He looked at the line he had written for a moment, and then he put the card in his bag and went down the stair for his breakfast. Kiva sat on the bed and opened and shut the hand, and all eight fingers that reported came in to the palm when she told them, and the two that did not came in with them.

Three nights. She had known it would be.

She took her book out from under the pillow. It was the first Sunday, and it went to Orla at four.

---

Orla Dane read the book on Sunday afternoon on her fish crate at the foot of the gate mast, with her stick hooked on the crate's edge and her bad knee straight out in front of her.

The gate yard was half empty. The boards were wet. Noa's reading desk stood under the mast with a tarpaulin over it, and the queue was gone, and a Board clerk was sweeping. She read slowly, as she always read the book, with her lips shut.

It was a long week in it. She had not had it since the Sunday before the road, a fortnight ago in the Old Yard, and it was all here, in the square pencil hand: the house of the Even Hand, and a Master who stood at a window, and a tin with two seals. The comb, and the seventh reed silent. The ferry.

*Friday the 27th. The ferry below the house of the Even Hand. Fever, from the middle of the river. A hundred and twelve, by Finch. Aske counted it all night. Broke Saturday at four. I can sit up.*

*Cold, the disk. Before and after. I looked.*

And on the right, where the reasons went, one line, small.

*I kept my mouth shut. I'd learned how.*

Orla read that three times.

She did not know what it was about. She knew the shape of it, at least, as one knows the shape of a thing under a sheet. She had read a book in a room in the Compact Library in 2664, with a Reader beside her and a pencil she was not let use, and there had been a line in it about a fever, and she had never told the girl what it said. The girl had read the same book in the sixth month, and had never told her.

She had not been told that the girl dreamed. Nobody had told her. And here was a line on the right of a page that said *I kept my mouth shut,* about a fever on a ferry, a mile from a Glory master's door.

She could ask. She was the sponsor. The book came to her by a Chair's order, and a sponsor could ask what was behind a line.

She did not ask. She sat on the crate with the book open on her knee and thought about why not.

It was not kindness. She had learned to distrust whatever reason she would have given aloud, and she would have said *kindness* aloud. It was that the girl had written *I'd learned how,* and that was a thing Orla had tried to teach a boy twenty years ago and had never managed. She had taught him everything else. She had built him a step at a time and explained every step, and she had never once let him keep anything back from her. She had thought that was what a sponsor was for.

The girl had learned it from a van, and a hem, and a man with burned hands. And she had written it down for her sponsor to read, plainly, so that the sponsor would know there was a door, and would know it was shut.

Orla shut the book.

She turned forward to Thursday, and the fifth, and the notch. *A notch at seven seconds. Every pen. Mr. Polglase says it's the harbour.* And Wednesday. *The lay. It has a gap after all. It's in the walking. I don't know if it's a stack. I know it's not a miss.*

She would give it back on Thursday, after the sixth, if the healer said yes. She would sign at six and not before.

She sat on the crate for some time after that. Then she got up, in two stages, and hooked the stick over her arm, and went down to the harbour.

---

The pump house was lit when she got there. It was a little after six, and the light was going, and the long hall glowed through its high windows like a chapel.

She did not go in. She had come to the door four times since Tuesday and not gone in. She went along the outside of it instead, by a stone walk with an iron rail, to the seaward side, where the pumps' outfalls poured into the basin on the count.

He was on the gallery. She had known he would be. He was at the north end of it, on an iron landing, with his back to her, looking down at a white board with black numbers on it. The tide was making. She could hear it coming in under the sea gate, a long way off, like breath.

She stopped ten paces from him.

She had thought about this for five months. She had thought about it on the transit, for a day and a half, with an officer of the Circuit snoring opposite. She had decided one thing in all that time, and she had told the Chair so. *I'll not use her to say it.* She had decided nothing else. She had found that she could not decide anything else until she saw his face.

He turned round.

He was forty. She had known that, and it was still a shock. She had last seen him at nineteen, in the infirmary behind the Measure Hall, with his right arm under a wire cage and a sheet, and his face turned to the wall. Now he was a tall stooped man with grey over his ears and a fitter's jacket, and his right hand was inside the jacket's front, and he was looking at her as one looks at weather coming.

"Dane," he said.

"Jessamy."

Nobody had called him that, she thought, in twenty years. She saw it land.

"You came by the transit," he said. "With the officers."

"My knee won't sit a cart."

"No. It never would." He did not move from the landing. "You've come to the door four times this week. Peverell told me. She thought you were from the Works, come to stop the pumps."

"I came to see you."

"I know what you came for."

She had planned what to say first. She had planned it on the transit. She was going to say that she had been wrong, plainly, without decoration. Then she was going to stop talking. She had decided that stopping was the hard part, and she had been right.

What she said instead was, "You should be at the gate."

He looked at her.

"They've a reader from the Chair's office at that mast," she said. "Bexley. He's reading every candidate with two doors, and he's reading a notch on every pen that nobody can explain. Their own man calls it the harbour. You know when the harbour's still, to the minute. You should take your book to that mast and sit with him and—"

She heard herself. She stopped.

It was the old thing. She had walked down the hill to tell him she had been wrong to do his thinking for him, and she had opened her mouth and told him what to do.

Hart said nothing at all for a moment. The tide came in under the sea gate, far off.

"I've been telling the Works for six months," he said. "Peverell's been telling them. Yeo's been telling them. The first who came was a woman with one hand, on Wednesday. Nobody's told me where I *should* be in twenty years." His voice did not rise. "You're the first. That's how I knew it was you, before you'd said my name."

"I didn't come to say that."

"No. You came to say you were wrong." He turned his watch on his wrist. "You wrote in the ninth month. Four lines. I read them on this landing more times than I'll tell you, and one of them was about her, and none of them said it." He looked at her. "You've come two hundred miles to say it. To my face. So that you'll have said it."

"Yes."

"Then say it."

She found that she could not. It was there, ready, in the words she had planned, and she opened her mouth and they were not the right ones any more. They were hers. They were for her. They would be said, and she would feel better, and he would stand on his landing with his hand in his coat exactly as he had stood before.

"She's—" said Orla.

She stopped. She stopped so hard that she felt it in her jaw.

He had heard it. She saw that he had. One word, and the beginning of the girl, and she had caught it and shut her teeth on it. But it had been said. It had been in the air between them for the length of a breath.

"There," said Hart, very quietly.

"I didn't—"

"I wondered," he said. "When her copy came, in the eleventh month, with three squares ruled off and one line left out on purpose. I wondered whether you'd come for me or for her. Whether you'd come to tell me about her. Whether you'd stand there and tell me what a good candidate she was, and how you'd done it right this time, and how she wrote everything down because you'd learned to let her." He was not angry. That was the worst of it. He said it as he would have read a gauge. "I wrote to her and not to you. You know why."

"Because I'd have answered."

"Because you'd have answered first." He turned back to the rail and the white board. "She came on Friday. She stood on this landing. She had a question behind her teeth that she wanted to ask me so badly that I could see it in her neck. She didn't ask it." He was quiet for a moment. "Did you teach her that?"

"No."

"No. I didn't think you had." He did not look round. "Good night, Dane."

Orla stood on the stone walk with her stick.

There was a thing she could have said then. It was short, and true, and it would have cost her something. She knew what it was. She had known for twenty years. She did not say it. She stood there until the light was gone off the water, and he did not turn round, and at last she went back along the walk, a step at a time, the way she had come.

At the corner of the pump house she stopped and looked back. He was a shape on the landing against the last of the sky, with one hand on the rail.

It had gone badly. She had known that it might. She had not known that the worst of it would be that he was right, about every word, and that she had said none of the right ones, and that she had come two hundred miles to say them.

She went up the hill to the officers' lodging in the dark, and did not light a lamp, and sat by the window with the book in her lap until it was time to sleep.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-20.md

# Chapter 20 — Kiva: Slack Water

The flood had been running for five hours when the kiddle shed its first rung.

Kiva was kneeling in the mud at the edge of the hard, with Pike on her left and the creek in front of her, black and fast and full to the brim. It was a little after eleven on Monday night. The tide was nearly at the top of its spring, and the whole of the Longshore had gone under water, so that the creek was no longer a creek. It was a river running the wrong way, inland, out of the sea, with the marsh grass streaming flat under it on both sides like hair.

The kiddle stood across the creek's mouth, forty paces off.

The Company crews had given it that name when they first saw one. It was the old coast word for a fish weir, the kind of fence of stakes that men had once driven across a creek to catch what the tide brought in. This was seven posts. They were grey, the height of a tall man above the water, and they stood in a line across the creek, five paces apart, with the flood pouring between them. Between each post and the next there hung a skin. It was grey-white and close-woven like canvas, and it went down into the water out of sight, and the flood went through it as water goes through a net.

Where the water went through, it went white. It came out on the landward side of the skin frosted, with a skin of ice on it as thin as paper, and the ice broke up and went away up the creek in plates. The cold came off it in a breath that Kiva could feel on her face from forty paces.

And the posts leaned. All seven leaned inland, with the flood, the way a line of men leans into a wind. When a big surge came in under the darkness, they leaned further, and the skins between them bellied out, white, and the water went through faster. When it eased, they came back an inch.

"Working," said Davy Penhale, beside her in the mud. "That's what we say. It's working the flood. It'll work the ebb the other way, when it turns."

He was the Company's catcher. The Board's warrant had put Graywater on Company ground, and the Company had sent its own man to catch, because Pike's hands were half a fist and a voice. Penhale was perhaps thirty, broad, red-bearded, in thigh boots and a canvas smock that had been black once. He talked all the time, low and cheerful. He had been talking since nine.

Kiva did not ask him what it was working for. She was a second. A second did not open her mouth until the bag was sealed, and there was no bag yet, and she had been told that on a van in Meridian in plainer words than she had ever been told anything. Penhale seemed to hear the question anyway.

"Lord knows what for," he said. "Same as a pump. Water comes in, water goes through, water goes out. My dad said they're only trying to be useful." He grinned in the dark. "He said that about my brother as well."

A surge came in.

It came in under the black water from the sea, a long low lift that Kiva felt in the mud under her knees before she saw it. The creek rose a hand. The seven posts leaned. The skins bellied, white, and the water through them went white, and the cold came off it in a gust.

And the fourth post, in the middle of the creek, cracked at the top.

It was a sound like a branch going in frost. The top of the post, a yard of it, came away and fell. It fell into the flood on the landward side of the skin. It was a heap of grey ash as it fell, a rung, as a rung comes off a string when a blade goes through the knot. Nobody had cut it. It had come off on its own, on the surge.

It hit the water and did not sink. It spread.

Kiva had never seen ash go into water. She saw it now. It went out on the black surface like milk poured into tea, grey, fast, in a long fan, and the flood took the fan and carried it inland up the creek, toward the hard, toward them. Where it went, the water steamed. The marsh grass under it went white.

"*Mace,*" said Rhea Sorn, from the causeway.

She did not raise her voice. She had been standing on the causeway behind them since nine, with her hands in her pockets, watching. Now one hand came out.

Doran Mace's line was on the creek's two banks, landward of the hard, three on each side. They had been standing there with their walls unlit for two hours. Now six walls came up together, silver, out over the black water. They did not stand on the water. They lay on it, flat, a hand deep in it, like a boom of logs lying across a river's mouth. They met in the middle, Tibb's from the north bank and Stroud's from the south, with a sound like two doors closing in another room.

The grey fan came up the creek and met the walls and stopped.

Most of it stopped. It piled against the silver like scum against a weir, steaming. But the walls lay only a hand deep in the water, and the flood was six feet deep, and some of the grey went under. Kiva saw it go. She saw it come up on the far side of the walls, thinner, and go away up the creek into the dark. It left a line of white in the grass on both banks as far as she could see.

"Hold it," said Mace. "Hold it, hold it. Stroud, down a hand. Tibb, down."

The walls went down. They lay deeper in the water. The grey stopped going under. It lay against the silver, steaming, and the flood pushed on it, and Mace's line leaned on their heels on the two banks and held.

---

"That's why you wait," said Penhale.

He said it quite quietly, with no cheer in it. He was looking at the line of white in the grass going away inland.

"That's the Board's ash, that is, gone up Gannet Creek to frost somebody's sheep. Twenty drams, maybe." He looked at the kiddle. "Can't cut on the flood. Can't cut on the ebb. Cut on the flood and the rung goes into the water and away inland. Cut on the ebb and it goes away out to sea and into the harbour mouth, and the Lord knows what that does to the Regiment." He sniffed. "You cut at the slack. Twenty minutes, at a spring. You cut every post you can in twenty minutes, and then you wait six hours, and you do it again."

"And if it sheds before?"

"Then you lose it, and you hold what you can, like Mace is holding." He jerked his chin at the walls. "Inland vans don't have that. A wall on water. We've had it on this coast two hundred years. You put your ward flat and you hold the loose stuff the way a boom holds oil. Your Mace learned it in two hours this afternoon." He sounded pleased. "He's quick, your Mace."

The rest of the shed rung lay against the walls in a grey crust. Two of the Company's men had come down the north bank with a long-handled net, like a shrimper's net, and were working it along the edge of the silver, scooping. What came up in the net was grey sludge, and it steamed, and they tipped it into a tin. It was not a bag. It was what could be saved.

Pike had not said anything. He was kneeling on Kiva's left, on a folded sack, with his bare grey hands on his knees and his eyes on the kiddle.

"It shed at the top of a surge," he said at last. "The fourth. The middle one. It leaned furthest."

"Yes."

"Then it'll shed again at the next big one, if there's a next big one before the slack." He turned his head and looked at her. "Don't tell me what the water's doing. You're a second; you've a mouth to keep. But if it changes, touch my sleeve. That's not talking."

She did not know what the water was doing. Nobody had ever asked her to know what the sea was doing.

But there was a pole beside her. It was one of Penhale's, an ash pole as thick as her wrist, driven into the mud at the creek's edge to carry the line of his first purse out across the water. It was solid. It went down into the mud and the mud went down to whatever was under the creek, and the creek went out to the kiddle, and to the sea.

She took her left hand out of her coat. It was warm; it had been warm since Sunday at seven. She laid the first two fingers on the pole, at a light touch, above the water.

*Something's pushing.*

Then, because she held them there and did not lean, the other thing came.

*It keeps time.*

It was slow. It was slower than the bollard on the quay. It came and went in long lifts, irregular, a long one and a short one and a long one. That was the flood, she thought, the surges, the thing that Jude had stood at the rail and counted and called *every count at once.* And under it, so faint that she was not sure, there was something else. Four and four.

Pru was on her right, kneeling on the line of the second purse with the cord round her fist. She took Kiva's right wrist without being asked and looked at the lamp on the causeway, since there was no clock, and counted under her breath.

"Seventy-six," she breathed. "You're quite calm."

So it was not her own pulse. It was the water. It surged, a long one and a short one, and under the surges, faint, there was the town. Kiva kept her mouth shut on all of it and kept her fingers on the pole.

---

The slack came at six minutes to midnight.

Kiva knew it before anybody saw it. She had kept the two fingers on the pole for most of an hour, with Pru taking her wrist every quarter, and the water had gone on keeping its long uneven time through the fingers into her hand. The surges had grown smaller. The long ones had grown shorter and the short ones had stopped. And then, quite suddenly, between one breath and the next, the fingers said nothing.

Not *pushing.* Not *keeps time.* Nothing at all.

She put her right hand on Pike's sleeve.

He did not look at her. He lifted his head and looked at the creek, and Kiva looked with him.

The water had not changed. It was still black and still full to the brim, and the marsh grass still lay flat under it. But the grass had stopped streaming. It lay quite still, like hair in a bath. The plates of ice that had been going away inland from the skins had stopped going anywhere; they turned slowly on the surface where they were. And the seven posts of the kiddle stood up.

They came upright together. They did not lean inland and they did not lean to sea. They stood straight up out of the black water like the stakes of a fence on a calm day, and the skins between them hung straight down. The white went out of the water that went through them, because no water was going through them. Everything was still.

It was the stillest thing that Kiva had ever seen. It was stiller than the courtyard at the house of the Even Hand. It was a whole creek mouth and a whole marsh and a mile of sea, all stopped at once.

"*Slack,*" said Penhale, aloud. "Six to twelve. Twenty minutes, near enough, if the wind holds."

"*Boat,*" said Rhea.

She was already in it. The Company had brought two of its own boats down on the roofs of its green vans, flat-bottomed punts with square ends, and one of them was at the foot of the hard with a Company man in it holding a pole. Rhea stepped into it as she stepped onto any floor. The Company man pushed off. The punt went out across the black still water toward the kiddle without a ripple, because there was nothing in the water to make one.

Penhale was on his feet. He had the line of his first purse in his two fists, and Pru had the second, and a Company man had the third. The lines went out from the hard across the water in three long curves and down into it below the first three posts. Under the water there, Kiva knew, there were three purses. They were sheets laced to iron rings, lying open on the creek bed under the posts, with a drawstring round each. They had been set at the last low water, at six that evening, by men in thigh boots standing in mud, before the tide had come in over them.

"First post," said Rhea, from the punt. She was standing in its bow, a pace from the post nearest the north bank. Her blade arm was down.

"*Not yet,*" said Pike.

He said it to Penhale, low and thick, as he had said it to Varley in a flat yard in Meridian. Penhale did not look round. Kiva saw his head go a little on one side, listening.

The post stood in the still water. Rhea's left heel came down flat on the punt's bottom boards.

"*Now,*" said Pike.

The yellow light went level across the creek, and the top of the first post came off and fell.

It fell straight down. There was no flood to take it and no ebb. It fell like a stone into still water, and the water closed over it with a grey bloom, and it went down out of sight, onto the purse lying open on the creek bed below.

"*Dark! One!*" said Penhale, and hauled.

The line came in through his fists, fast, hand over hand. Under the water, the drawstring would be closing the purse round the rung like the neck of a bag. He hauled until the line stopped coming, and then he put his weight back on it and held it there.

"*Closed,*" he said. "One fathom. Two fathom." He was counting. It was his own count, the coast's, and Kiva had never heard it before. "Three fathom. Shut. It's shut."

The rung was in a closed purse at the bottom of a creek, under six feet of still water, and nobody could touch it until the tide went out. But it was not loose. It was not going anywhere.

"*Second post,*" said Rhea.

---

The second went as the first had gone. Pike said *not yet* and *now,* and Rhea cut, and the rung went down through the still water, and Pru hauled.

She hauled well. Kiva watched her and was proud and did not say so. Pru had never hauled a drawstring in her life, and she had been shown how by Penhale at six that evening, once, in the mud. She took it in hand over hand with the cord round her fist and her face scarlet in the lamplight, and when the line stopped she leaned back on it with all her weight, as he had.

"*Shut,*" said Pru. "I think. It's stopped."

"It's shut," said Penhale. "I felt it from here."

"*Third,*" said Rhea.

The punt moved along the line of posts. The Company man poled it without a sound. Rhea stood in the bow in front of the third post, the one nearest the middle, and the skin between the third post and the fourth hung down beside her into the black water like a curtain.

Kiva was not watching Rhea.

She was watching the skin. She was watching the far side of it, the sea side, where it went down into the water out of the light. That was the far edge. The skin hung between two posts. When one post was cut, the skin on that side would have nothing to hang from. On a wharf in Meridian a skin with nothing on it had tried to fold over a catcher. On a level yard it had tried to fold an officer. She did not know what a skin did in a creek. She did not think anybody here knew. The Company had only seen kiddles since the autumn.

"*Not yet,*" said Pike.

Her fingers were still on the pole. They said nothing. Still nothing. The slack held.

"*Now.*"

The top of the third post came off. It fell straight down into the still water, and Penhale's Company man hauled the third line.

And the skin between the third post and the fourth lifted at its far edge.

It lifted out of the water. It came up on the sea side of the kiddle in a long grey fold, streaming, a yard of it and then two, and it bent over, slowly, as a page turns. It was folding over the cut post toward the line that ran from the third purse to the hard, toward the man hauling on it. The water ran off it in sheets, frosted, white.

"*Far edge,*" said Kiva.

Two words. She did not say them loudly. She said them as Pike had taught her to say them, short, with the swallow, so that they went across forty paces of still water to the only person who needed them.

The Company man let go of the line.

He let go and stepped back on the hard, as Rhea had stepped back off a skin in the Corn Exchange yard, without turning round. The fold came over and came down. It came down on the water where the line had been, flat, with a slap, and lay there steaming. The line went slack under it. The purse at the bottom of the creek, below the third post, was half drawn and no more.

"*Hold,*" said Rhea, from the punt.

She did not cut the skin. Kiva saw her look at it and decide. She leaned out of the bow and put the flat of her lit blade on the fold where it lay on the water, not the edge, only the flat, as Carrow had put hers on Jude in a lane. The skin lay down under it. It went grey, and thin, and was still.

"*Line,*" said Rhea.

Pru had already let go of her own. She went down the hard on her knees in the mud to where the third line lay slack, and took it up, and hauled. It came in. It came in slowly, from under the skin lying on the water, and then it stopped.

"*Shut,*" said Pru. "It's—I think it's shut. It's heavy."

"One fathom," said Penhale, beside her. He had his hand over hers on the line. "Two fathom. Three. It's shut." He looked at her sideways. "You'd make a coast catcher, miss, if you'd stand the wet."

Pru went scarlet to the ears. She did not let go of the line.

---

The slack ended at fourteen minutes past midnight.

Kiva knew it a breath before anybody else. The two fingers on the pole had said nothing for twenty minutes. And then they said *pushing,* faint, and then *keeps time.* It was a long lift, very slow, outward, toward the sea. And she looked at the water, and the water had not moved. The grass lay still. The ice plates turned where they were. Nothing had changed that she could see.

She put her hand on Pike's sleeve again.

He looked at the creek. Nothing had moved.

Then the marsh grass on the near bank stirred. It stirred very slightly, all together, the other way, toward the sea. And the seven posts, four with their tops and three without, began to lean. They leaned the other way, out, as the water under them began to go back the way it had come.

"*Ebb,*" said Penhale. "Twelve fourteen. Out of the boat, officer, if you please. It'll work the ebb now till dawn." He took his hands off the first line. "Three purses shut. On this coast a shut purse is a sealed bag, miss. We tie them at the low water for the Board's sake." He looked sideways at Kiva. "You may talk now, if you've a mind to. I've been talking for both of us."

Rhea was already coming back. The punt slid across the water to the hard, and the water was moving under it now, and the Company man had to push. Rhea stepped out onto the stone.

She did not look at Kiva. She took her slate out of her pocket and wrote on it under the causeway lamp, quite fast, in the middle of the work, with four posts still standing behind her in the creek.

"Three," she said. "Three posts in the slack. One shed on the flood and held on Mace's walls, part of it. Four to stand till low water. A fold on the third that came off the far edge onto the line, and the second said so, and the line came off it." She did not look up. "And the second had the slack's end by the hand, a breath before the grass. Pike."

"She touched my sleeve," said Pike.

"Did you know it before she did?"

"No." Pike's hands lay on his knees. "I knew it by the grass, like anybody."

Rhea wrote it down.

Kiva had taken her fingers off the pole. She knelt in the mud and looked at them, the first two of her left hand, which bent and gripped and reported pressure and nothing else. She had told herself all winter that they were a loss with a little left over.

She thought of a man on an iron landing with a book of slacks in his pocket. *Ache. Quiet. Ache.* He had laid a dead hand on a grating twice a day for six months. And the bad hand had known, before anything in the harbour moved, when the water stopped and when it started again.

"It went quiet," she said to Pike, under her breath. The bags were sealed; she could say it now. "Through the pole. When the tide stopped, it went quiet. All of it, the surges and the town under them. And it came back before the water moved."

Pike looked at her. It was the long considering look that she had seen him give a hem.

"Then you've a gauge," he said. "I'd not have thought it. Two fingers that tell you nothing, and they tell you that." He nearly smiled. "Write it down before you sleep."

---

At twenty past one, a lantern came along the causeway from the direction of the Mole.

It came slowly, swinging, and it limped. Kiva knew who it was before the light reached the lamp on the hard. It was Jude, with his left arm in its linen and his right hand holding the lantern up, and his coat buttoned to the chin against the wind off the flats. He came down the hard to where Rhea stood, and stopped, and held out a paper.

"Officer," he said. "From Seven. I'd want it said that I'm sorry."

Rhea took the paper and read it under the causeway lamp.

It was Noa's hand. Kiva could see that from where she knelt; nobody else wrote so narrow and so upright. Rhea read it twice, and then she held it out to Kiva without a word.

*By order of the Clerk of the Measure, at the protest of the host circuit: every candidate of the Meridian circuit who stands under the rule is to be read on the gate mast at six this morning, in the presence of both readers, before the Clerk rules on the host's entries. A candidate absent is struck from the seeding. Rowan, K., stands under the rule. N.B.*

And under it, in Perrin's large round hand, pressed hard:

*Seven calls her. I'm sorry. P.C., captain.*

"Six," said Kiva.

"Six," said Jude. "It went up on the tent at half past eleven. The Ropewalk asked for it. They said that if Meridian's reader could read their people in front of their town, their reader could read Meridian's in front of the Board." He scratched his jaw with the lantern hand, so that the light swung. "It's fair. Cade said it was fair. He said it twice, and then he wrote that, and then he sent me."

Kiva looked at Rhea.

The officer stood on the hard with the slate in one hand and Noa's paper in the other. Behind her, in the creek, four grey posts leaned out to sea on the ebb, with the black water going through the skins between them and coming out white. There were four rungs still to come off them at low water, at dawn. There would be four lines to haul and four far edges, and Pike to call *not yet* and *now* to a catcher who could not see the tops of the posts from the water.

*If T7 is called on a night of mine, she is T7's and I go short.*

Rhea had written that. She had written it in the autumn, on a slate-blue card, and read it aloud to four people in a kitchen, and they had all signed it on the back.

"Go," said Rhea.

"There's four posts—"

"There's four posts and I've a catcher and a voice and a Company boat and six walls on the water. I'll go short." Rhea folded Noa's paper and gave it back to her. "That's what the card says. I wrote it so that I'd not have to decide it at one in the morning on a mud hard. I'm not deciding anything. I'm reading you what I wrote."

Kiva got up out of the mud. Her knees were stiff and wet through.

Pru had got up too. She stood on the hard with the cord still round her fist and the third line at her feet, and she looked from Kiva to Rhea and back again.

"I'm not read," said Pru. "I've one door. Nobody's calling me." She went red and kept going. "I'm the cord. If the officer will have me, I'll stay. I'd like to haul the four at dawn. I've only hauled two."

"Three," said Penhale.

"Three. I'd like to haul seven."

Rhea looked at her for a moment.

"I'll have you," she said. "Gladly."

Then she turned to Kiva, and did a thing that Kiva had never seen her do. She put her hand on Kiva's shoulder, lightly, for a moment, the way one steadies a person on a stair.

"I allow myself one piece of advice a season," she said. "I spent the winter's in the first month. This is the spring's, and it's the only one you'll get." She took her hand away. "When you're called, go. Don't look back at the creek."

---

They walked back along the Longshore causeway in the dark, Kiva and Jude, with the lantern.

The causeway was old, a raised track of stone across the flats, wide enough for a cart. On their left the marsh went away inland, black, with the tide going off it. On their right was the sea. It was a long way out now and going further, a pale line under the sky. Between the causeway and the sea there was nothing but flat wet sand. It shone faintly where the sky lay on it.

Kiva did not look back at the creek. She found that it was hard not to, and that she could do it.

Jude did not say anything for a long time. He walked on the seaward side with the lantern in his good hand, limping, and he looked at the sea the whole way.

"It's going out," he said at last.

"It's the ebb."

"I know it's the ebb. Penhale told me. He told me at six, when I brought the sacks." He did not turn his head. "I've never seen it go out. From the boat it just was. It was there all the time. Now there's a mile of sand where it was at eleven, and it's still going."

"It'll come back."

"At dawn. And then it'll go again. Twice a day, for ever." He walked a few paces. "Yeo said it was always the same thing. What ash is for. Keeping that out." He lifted the lantern a little toward the pale line. "I've been thinking about it since Saturday. I'd want to know what it's like to do that. Not for a lane. Not for a board. For a kitchen."

Kiva looked at him.

He had asked what ash was for of everybody since the autumn. He had asked it of a Marshal on a moor and a foreman on a scarp and his own four on a shed step, and he had been given a hand and a floor and a cell. He had never sounded like this, asking. He sounded as though he had found the answer and did not know yet what it would cost him.

"You could ask her," she said. "Yeo."

"I could." Jude walked on. "Not yet. There's a Measure first. I'm on the book for it, and I signed the warrant, and Cade's captain." He was quiet for a moment. "I'd want it said that I thought about it, though. On a causeway, at two in the morning. In case anybody asks."

"I'll say it."

"Good," said Jude. He did not look away from the sea.

---

Rhea Sorn stood on the Gannet Creek hard at twenty past six on Tuesday morning, with the light coming grey off the flats and four posts standing in the mud in front of her, and no second.

The tide was out. The creek was a creek again, a narrow thread of water a yard wide running through the bottom of a great channel of grey mud. The marsh grass lay flat and wet on both banks, white in a long line where the shed rung had gone over it at eleven. The kiddle stood across the channel. Its four uncut posts stood up out of the mud like the stakes of a fence. Its three cut ones were stumps. Between them hung the skins, grey, slack, with their lower edges lying in the mud.

They were leaning seaward. The ebb was nearly done. In a few minutes they would stand up straight.

The Company's closing crew was on the south bank: four men with a closing licence and their own officer, a big slow woman called Bray with a voice like a foghorn. They had been there since five. They were waiting for the posts to come down so that they could get at the tear. It lay in the creek bed under the fourth post, the middle one, under a yard of mud and a hand of water, and nobody could close it while the kiddle stood on it.

Penhale was in the channel, in the mud to his knees, in front of the fourth post. He had reset the purses at the end of the ebb, four of them, on the mud, open, with their lines up the bank. Pru had the line of the fourth. She had not slept. She was sitting on a sack on the hard with the cord round her fist and her eyes on the post. She had said four words since three o'clock. One of them had been *please,* when Purvis gave her his coat.

And Pike was on the hard on his knees, on his folded sack, at the edge of the mud, a yard from where the girl had knelt. Rhea had seen him go there at six. He had not asked.

"I'll have the far edge," he had said. "I've hands enough to point."

"And the voice?"

"And the voice." He had not looked at her. "I'll do both. I did both for twenty years before there was ever a second in this van."

She had let him. She had written it on the slate, under *Before,* in the middle of the night. *Second gone to her four, by the card. The catcher's voice takes the far edge as well. One pair of eyes for two places.* And she had looked at the line, and at Pike's grey hands lying on his knees, and had not crossed it out.

The posts stood up.

They came upright together, all four, out of the mud, as they had come upright in the still water at midnight. The thread of the creek in the channel's bottom stopped running. It lay in its bed like a ribbon. Low water slack: a quarter past six by Rhea's watch, and the morning very quiet, with a curlew calling a long way off over the flats.

She went down into the channel.

She cut the fifth post standing in the mud beside it, with her boots sunk to the ankle. Pike said *not yet* and *now* to Penhale, low. The rung came down onto the open purse in the mud, and Penhale's man drew it, and it was shut. The sixth went the same way. On the sixth the skin's far edge stirred, a hand, and lay down. Pike watched it and said nothing, because it lay down.

The seventh went clean.

That left the fourth, the middle one, the tall one that had shed at the top of the surge. It stood over the tear. Its skin hung from it on both sides, to the third stump and the fifth, and lay in the mud on the sea side in a long grey sheet.

Rhea went to it. Penhale stood in front of it, below, in the mud, with his purse open at his knees and the thread of the creek running past a yard to his left. It was not running now. It lay still.

"*Not yet,*" said Pike.

Rhea looked up at the top of the post. She did not look at Pike. She had never needed to. On every floor for six years she had had a catcher who watched her heel and a second who watched the far edge, and she had cut when the voice behind her said *now.* She waited for it.

It did not come.

She waited a count. She heard Pike draw breath. She looked round.

He was not looking at her heel. He was looking past her, past the post, at the sea side, where the skin lay in the mud. Its far edge had lifted. It was coming up out of the mud in a long grey lip, streaming, as the third skin had come up out of the water at midnight. It was turning over toward the purse, toward Penhale standing in the mud with nobody watching for him.

"*Far edge,*" said Pike.

Penhale stepped back. He stepped back to the left, out of the fold's way, which was the only way to step in a channel of mud. The fold came over and came down where he had been, flat, on the mud and on the edge of the open purse, half on it and half off.

And Pike said, a count late, because his eyes had been on the far edge and not on the heel, "*Now.*"

Rhea cut.

She knew as she cut that it was late. The fold lay across half the purse's mouth. The rung came off the top of the fourth post and fell straight down, because the water was still, onto the purse and onto the fold lying across it. Half of it went in. Half of it hit the grey skin and slid off it, sideways, into the creek's thread a yard to Penhale's left.

The thread was still. For a moment the grey lay in it like a spill of flour in a gutter.

Then the slack ended. The thread stirred. It began to run, very slowly, inland, as the flood came back. The grey went with it in a long fan. Where it went, the mud went white.

"*Shut,*" said Pru, on the hard. She was hauling the fourth line hand over hand. "It's—it's light. It's shut. It's light."

"*Mace,*" said Rhea.

The walls came down on the thread. Six of them, across the channel, flat on the water, a hand deep. They held what they could. The Company's net went along the edge of the silver. It came up grey and steaming.

---

She wrote the slate at seven, on the hard, with the flood coming in over the mud behind her and the Company's crew down in the channel at the tear. The kiddle was gone. Its seven stumps had gone to ash in the mud, and the skins with them, in a long grey film that the tide was lifting off the channel's floor. Bray stood over the tear with her hands out, and her crew round her, and the light under their hands was steady and white.

*Closed: a quarter to seven, by the Company, on the Board's warrant.*

*Lives: none.*

*Works: the marsh grass of Gannet Creek, frosted for a quarter mile inland. A sheep.*

*Tinned: six bags, and the net's tin. The sixth bag light.*

She did not write the figure. Penhale would weigh it at the Company's yard. She knew what it would be, near enough, and she knew what the fourth rung would have weighed if it had all gone into the purse.

*Remarks: three.*

*The officer cut three posts in the high water slack and four in the low, from a boat and from the mud. A post shed a rung at the top of the flood before the slack, uncut. Held in part on walls laid flat on the water. That remark is the creek's.*

*The second called the far edge once, at midnight, and the line came off it. The second also had the slack's end by the hand, a breath before the grass. That is entered for whoever reads this.*

She stopped. She looked out over the flats, where the sea was coming back in a long pale line, faster than a man could walk.

*At one in the morning the second was called to her four by the card. The officer went short. At low water the catcher's voice took the far edge as well, by his own wish and with the officer's leave. One pair of eyes for two places. He called the far edge and the catcher came off it. He called the cut a count late, because his eyes were where they ought to have been. Half a rung went into the creek. That remark is the officer's. She wrote the card.*

She read it over. She did not cross anything out.

Pike was sitting on the tailboard of the Company's van with his hands on his knees. They were shaking. He had not said anything since the bags were shut. Pru was beside him, asleep, sitting up, with her head against the van's side and Purvis's coat round her and the cord still round her fist.

"It was mine," said Pike, when Rhea came. "The count late."

"It was the card's. I wrote it." She hung the slate on her belt. "You watched the right place."

"She'd have had the heel and the far edge both. Two of us. One each." He looked at his hands. "I used to do both. Twenty years. I don't know when I stopped being able to."

"You stopped when you had a second," said Rhea. "So did I. That's what a second's for. You don't know you've stopped till she's gone." She sat down beside him on the tailboard, which she did not often do. "She went. I told her to. I'd do it again."

"I know you would."

They sat for a while and watched the tide come in over Gannet Creek. Bray's crew came up out of the channel with their hands dark. The thread of water became a creek, and then a channel full of grey water, and then the flats were going under again for the second time since midnight. It would go on doing that for ever, Rhea thought, whatever anybody tinned.

Half a rung. Twenty drams, perhaps, of the Board's warrant, gone into Gannet Creek. And a remark under her own name on a coast slate that a Company officer would read.

She had written that card in the autumn, at a kitchen table that was not hers, for a girl she had not yet decided she wanted. She had written it so that the girl's four would always have the first word. She had not thought about what it would cost to keep, because she had not yet had to keep it.

It had cost half a rung. She found that she did not mind as much as she would have expected, and that she minded something else a great deal more. She had stood in the mud with a blade on her arm and waited for a voice behind her, and the voice had been late. For one count she had been an officer with nobody watching the far edge but a man with half a fist, doing two things at once. She had known exactly how much she had come to lean on a girl of seventeen who was not hers.

The card ended on the thirtieth. *Then we look again, and either of us may say no.*

She took the slate off her belt and looked at it once more, and put it back, and said nothing to anybody about that at all.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-21.md

# Chapter 21 — Kiva: Read

The gate mast was lit at six on Tuesday morning by four lamps on poles, and there were more people round it than Kiva had seen at any reading in her life.

She had slept for three hours. She had come up Quay Street at two with mud to her knees and Jude's lantern going out. Aske had been awake in the bed by the door, and he had said nothing, and had held her wrist for a minute in the dark, and then turned over. Now she stood on the boards of the timber pond in a clean skirt with her hair wet from the pump. The sky was grey over the Graving's glass roof, and she could still feel the creek's cold in the bones of her knees.

The Ropewalk had come. All of it, it seemed: the first four in front, Carrow with her arms folded and Lyle half a pace behind, and behind them a crowd in green. They did not say anything. They had not come to say anything. They had come to watch Meridian be read in front of them, as their own people had been read in front of the town a week ago.

The Meridian fours stood on the other side. Halden License was there, all four, with Kellan in the black and gold, and Old Four, and Twenty-Eight with Dray on her stick at the end. Orla was on her crate. Mara was not there; Kiva had not expected her to be.

And there was a small woman in the Board's grey, with a gold chain round her neck and her hands folded in front of her, standing a little apart from everybody. Her face was the kind that has been shut in a great many rooms.

"The Clerk," said Perrin, under his breath. "Of the Measure. That's her."

"I know."

"I'd like it known that I've never seen her smile. Talley says she smiled on Saturday. I don't believe him."

Noa was at his desk with his tape threaded. Polglase was at his, a yard away. Neither of them had looked at the crowd.

"Rowan, K., Meridian," said a clerk with a list. "Under the rule."

Perrin stepped forward to the desks. That was his place now. He had read it in the Chair's conditions, and he had said it at the fifth crossing, and he said it now, loud and level, so that the green crowd could hear.

"*Rowan. Stance. One door, declared, by her captain's voice.*"

A murmur went through the green. Kiva understood it. They had heard of the girl with three doors. They had come to see three doors.

She went to the cross.

She did not do anything clever. She stood with her weight even, as Carrow had stood, and opened her still door, and it went down through the boards into the black water under them. It went down through the water into the mud, and through the mud into whatever the timber pond had been dug from, and it held. The pen dropped and drew one line.

"One door declared," said Noa. "One line."

"One line," said Polglase. He looked at his tape, and then at her over his steel spectacles, with a kind of mild curiosity. "That's all she has open, Mr. Bexley?"

"That's all she's let open. On any board. It's on her certificate."

"And the other two?"

"Shut," said Noa. "She has a crossing certified. Ember to still. She's read for that on this mast when she makes one, which is one in seven days and no faster. She made one on Thursday. You read it."

"I did." Polglase wrote. "A notch at seven seconds." He looked up again. "I've been thinking about your notch, Mr. Bexley. I've decided I don't like it."

"Nor do I," said Noa.

Kiva shut. She stepped off the cross.

The Clerk had not moved. She stood with her hands folded and looked at the two tapes, at the single line on each, and said nothing at all. Then she inclined her head an inch toward the clerk with the list, as a person does who has seen what she came to see.

"That's the candidate under the rule," said the clerk with the list. "The reading's done."

"No," said Perrin.

---

Everybody looked at him.

He had gone red. He had known that he would, and he did not seem to mind it. He stood by the reading desks with his big hands at his sides and the coil of Coombe's rope over one shoulder, where it had been since the moor.

"I'd like a thing read," he said. "Under my own name. It isn't her. It's me and Pryce."

"Mr. Cade," said Noa. His voice had gone careful.

"You know what it is. You've got it in your notebook with two questions after it." Perrin did not look away from him. "The Marshal's floor. The twelfth of last month, in the Pit at home. Pryce had a wall up with a string leaning on it, and she was walking it, and I planted at her back with my shoulder in her. My still door and her ward on one thing, agreeing.
