A lodger came up off the race at it, and Rowan said *nobody,* and I came off." He drew a breath as far as his ribs would let him. "There was a mast on that floor. It was the Board's. Nobody's ever said what it read. Nobody's ever said whether that was a stack, or whose, or whether it'd be an incident on Rowan's card because it was her ground I was supposed to be on and wasn't."

The crowd in green had gone very quiet.

"I've been thinking about it for three weeks," said Perrin. "I've said *nobody stands together* on every floor since. I'd like to know what I've been saying it about. I'd like it read on a sanctioned mast, with two readers, in front of the Clerk, and then I'll know." He turned his head. "Pryce?"

Anwen came out of the crowd. She did not hurry.

"I want it said I asked for it too," she said. "On the boat. He said wait till there was a mast with two readers on it. There's one."

The Clerk of the Measure looked at Perrin for a long moment. Her face did not change.

"It's two doors on one thing," she said. Her voice was dry and very precise, like a pencil on good paper. "From two candidates. Neither of whom is under the rule."

"Yes, ma'am."

"Then it's not a reading under the rule. It's a reading at your request." She unfolded her hands. "Will you pay for the ash, Mr. Cade?"

"It's on our book. Talley's got it."

"Then read it," said the Clerk to the two desks. "And write down what it is."

---

They did it on the boards under the mast, between the two white crosses, with the whole of the Ropewalk watching.

Anwen put up her wall. It came up at her hip, low and silver, the width of a door, and stood. Perrin went behind her. He did not plant at once. He looked at Kiva first.

Kiva knew why. She had been watching the timber pond's black water since she came onto the boards, and the old wall of the Graving beside it, and the mud at the pond's edge where the boards ended. On the Longshore there had been a lodger in every bit of tidal mud, by what Coombe said. She did not see one here. She did not see anything lying along any edge that looked like a crack in the coping.

"Clean," she said. "As far as I can see."

"As far as you can see," said Perrin. "Right."

He planted. His still door went into the boards a pace behind Anwen, and he leaned his shoulder into her back, as he had in the Pit, and her wall stopped shaking. The two of them stood there. A ward and a stance on one thing, agreeing.

The pen dropped. It drew one line.

It was one firm single line, as Carrow's had been, as Lyle's had been.

"One line," said Noa.

"*Laid,*" said Polglase. Then he stopped, and looked at his tape again, and frowned. "Between two. Well."

"Shut," said Kiva.

Perrin shut and stepped back. Anwen let her wall down. The pen came up off the tape.

Noa stood at his desk with his pencil not moving. Everybody was looking at him, and he did not seem to know it. He was looking at the tape as he had looked at the notch on Thursday.

"Well?" said Perrin.

"It's one line," said Noa. "On a sanctioned tape, with two readers. That's what it reads." He put the pencil down. "It isn't Rowan's. She's not in it; she's on the boards with nothing open. So it's no incident of hers, and it never was, on the Marshal's floor or anywhere. That's the first thing. I'd like it said, because I've had it in my notebook for three weeks and I've not been sure."

"And the second?"

"It's not a stack," said Noa. "Not under any rule that I know. Every rule that I know, ours and the Board's, says a stack is a person's two doors. This is two people. It's not anybody's. There's no column for it." He looked at Polglase. "Is there one in yours?"

"No," said Polglase. "We lay in one body. We don't teach two. I've seen it done on boats, by fishermen, holding a net in a sea." He took off his spectacles and rubbed them. "I'd not have called it anything. I'd have called it two people holding a net."

"The tape can't tell the difference," said Noa. "It doesn't know whether it's one person with two doors or two people with one each. It's one line either way." He picked up his notebook. "I can't tell either. Not from the tape."

He wrote. Kiva could see it, because she had come to the desk and nobody had stopped her. It was under the line that had been there since the second month: *Two persons, unlike doors, one load, agreeing: one line on the tape. Q.: is it a stack? Q.: whose? I do not know. I am not going to say.*

He wrote under it, in the same narrow hand.

*Saltmere gate mast, the 8th of the third month, at the captain's request, two readers. A.: one line, sanctioned. Not hers. Not a stack under any rule; a stack is one person's. Whose: both. The tape does not know the difference. The lodger did.*

He looked at the last sentence for a while. Then he left it.

"That's what I'll say," he said to Perrin. "It's yours, if you want it. It's in my book."

Perrin let out a long breath.

"Then *nobody stands together* is right," he said. "It's not a rule about Rowan. It's not a rule about stacks. It's a rule about a thing in the mud that can't tell the difference either." He looked round at the green crowd, at Carrow and Lyle, and said it to them, quite plainly. "That's what I'll keep saying. On every floor. I'd like it known why."

Carrow had not moved. But Lyle had. She had come a half pace forward, out of the crowd, and she was looking at Anwen with that square careful face, and Anwen was looking back.

---

The Clerk's ruling went up on the tent pole at noon, under the Board's seal, in a clerk's hand.

*The readings made under the rule stand on the tape as readings. Whether a reading is an incident is for the candidate's own circuit and its rules. A lay is entered in the Saltmere circuit's column, as taught there, and is not an incident for any candidate of the host circuit. The conditions of the Meridian Placement Chair bind the candidates of Meridian. Every reading made under the rule, at the gate and on any board of the Measure, is to be posted where the draft boards and the Board may read it.*

*At the request of a captain, a reading was also made this morning of two candidates of Meridian, with two unlike doors on one thing. It read as one line. It is entered as a reading. It is in no circuit's column. By order.*

"*In no circuit's column,*" said Jude. He had come up from the loft at noon, still yawning. "That's a new one."

"It's a true one," said Anwen.

Carrow was at the tent pole when Kiva went to read it. She had read it already. She stood with her arms folded and her eyes on the line about the lay.

"Well," she said, without turning round. "We're in our own column."

"You always were."

"We were in it in our town. Now we're in it in the Board's ledger, in a Clerk's hand, under a Meridian rule." Carrow turned round. Her wind-brown face was quite serious. "Your captain stood behind his Hold this morning to be read. In front of us. In front of the whole school, when he'd no need to, so as to know what he'd been shouting about." She was quiet for a moment. "We'd not have done that. We'd not have thought there was anything to know."

"There's a thing in the mud on your Longshore," said Kiva. "Coombe calls it a lodger. It goes to two doors agreeing."

"I know. Maud told me last night. Your Hold told her." Carrow looked at her steadily. "Wenna had a bad night. That's what we say. I've laid on the flats every spring since I was fourteen, and so has Maud, and we've both still got two." She unfolded her arms. "I'll think about it. I'll not stop. I'll think about it."

"That's all anybody asked."

"No," said Carrow. "Your captain asked to be read. That's a good deal more." She went away across the boards.

---

Mara Rowan stood on the outfall gallery at ten to six on Tuesday morning, with her reading hand in her coat pocket.

She had promised herself she would not lay that hand on anything in this town until she had decided what she was going to do with it. She had been in Saltmere since Wednesday night, and she had not decided. She had read Hart's book of slacks and Peverell's fault columns and the sea gate's table, and she had found the three-and-twenty minutes. She had sat at the keeper's desk every night until midnight with her pencil. She had not touched a cell.

"It's quiet," said Hart.

He was on the iron landing below the gallery, at the grating, with his right hand out of his jacket and laid flat on the iron bars. His left hand held his watch up where he could see it. The pumps were running. Behind the wall at Mara's back, six iron beetles went up and down on the count, and the outfalls poured into the basin below her in four great gulps and four. She could feel it in the rail through her coat sleeve.

"It's quiet," said Hart again. "Twelve past six."

"The table says the slack at the sea gate's at ten to," said Yeo, beside Mara. "And three-and-twenty on top—"

"Thirteen past," said Mara. "He's a minute early."

"He's never early," said Peverell, from the end of the gallery. "The table's late."

There were five of them on the gallery. Peverell, small and upright, in her keeper's apron, with her spectacles on a cord. Yeo, in green with gold on her collar. Rusk, at the far end, with his thumbs in his waistcoat and his eyes half shut, as though he were asleep, which he never was. Hart on the landing below. And Mara.

The tide was out. It was the bottom of a spring ebb, and the basin had drained away from the pump house's foot until there was nothing below the outfalls but a hand of brown water lying on grey mud. The tide staff stood beside the grating with its black numbers going up from *0* at the mud. Ten paces out from the outfalls, in the middle of the mud, there was a place where the water lay a little darker.

It was rising.

Mara saw it come up. She had been told what she would see, by Hart and by Peverell and by Yeo, all three, in plain words. It made no difference. She saw it come up out of the mud as a thing comes up out of a dream, slowly, with the mud sliding off it in sheets.

It was a ring. It was grey, the grey of ash, with a rim of white frost along its lip. It was as wide as a cart is long, and it rose out of the mud straight up, all round at once, like the rim of a great barrel being pushed up from below. It rose a foot, two feet, three. The hand of brown water round it went white where it touched the ring's outside and stiffened into ice. It rose four feet.

It stopped.

"Four foot six," said Hart, from the landing. He had a fitter's level in his left hand, a brass tube with a bubble in it, and he was sighting across it at the staff. "And a half inch. At the springs a fortnight back, four foot one."

Inside the ring there was water.

It was brown harbour water, level with the ring's lip, four and a half feet above the mud. It should have run out. There was nothing to hold it but a ring of grey that stood on the mud like a wall round a pond. But it was held. It stood up inside the ring, flat and brown and full, four and a half feet higher than the water outside, as though the ring were a cup and somebody had filled it.

And its surface moved.

It trembled. It trembled in rings, very small, from the middle outward to the lip, and it did it four times, and stopped. Four times, and stopped. Mara watched it, and then she took out her watch and watched it with that, and she did not need to. She knew the count. She had been listening to it in the rail through her sleeve for an hour, and in the pump house floor for a week, and in the stones of the low town since Wednesday.

"It's the bank's," she said.

"It's the bank's," said Hart. "To the stroke. I've timed it at every spring since the tenth month."

The outfalls below her went on pouring into the basin, four and four, in step with the ring. The pumps behind her went up and down. And Hart's right hand lay flat on the grating, and he said it was quiet.

"How," said Mara.

"I don't know." He did not look up. "That's the third column of my book. *Quiet.* Every slack. The pumps going, the outfalls going, the hand quiet. And at the springs, when the water's low enough to see, that." He lifted the level an inch toward the ring. "It's as if the count goes out of the harbour at the slack and into that, and nowhere else. I don't know if that's what it is. It's what it looks like."

"The children call it the Hoop," said Yeo. Her voice was flat. "In the low town. They come down to the wall at the springs to see it. They think it's the Regiment's. A sort of drum."

"It's not in my fault column," said Peverell. "Nothing's at fault. The pumps are sound. The cells are sound. It's not a fault for a ring to come up out of the mud at the bottom of a spring tide and stand there." She was holding the rail very hard. "It's in my remarks. Every one. Since the twenty-sixth of the ninth month, a hand high. My first keeper taught me what goes in remarks."

Rusk had opened his eyes. He was looking at the ring with an expression that Mara had never seen on him, and she had seen him look at a great many things. He did not say anything. He took a pencil out of his waistcoat and wrote in a small book with a black cover.

The ring stood for twenty-one minutes.

At six thirty-five Hart said, "Ache," and the ring went down.

It went down as it had come up, all round at once, into the mud. The water inside it came over its lip as it sank. It came out in a sheet on all sides, brown and then white, frosting as it went, and spread across the mud in a long flat wave and was gone. The ring went down into the mud and the mud closed over it. There was a place that lay a little darker, and then there was nothing.

The flood was coming in under the sea gate. Mara could hear it, a long way off, like breath.

---

Peverell took them into the keeper's room behind the desk at seven, and shut the door, and took a book down from the press.

It was the press that every pump house had, Mara thought. There had been one like it at Ferren, behind the three pumps, with three centuries of books in calf gone black. This one was bigger. It went from floor to ceiling, and the books in it went back to 2398, when the pump house was built.

"You asked me on Thursday," said Peverell, "whether there was a night. Before the ninth month. A night when everything in this house did something together that was in no column." She laid the book on the table and did not open it. "You said Ferren had one. In 2604. You said a keeper there had a fever and wrote it in remarks because the needles did it too."

"Yes."

"I said I'd look. I looked." Peverell put her hand flat on the calf. "I didn't look for 2604. I looked for every remark in every book that said the whole house did one thing. There's not many. Three hundred years, and four." She opened the book at a straw. "This is one. I didn't know the date till I'd found it. Then I saw that it was yours."

Mara looked at the spine. *2604.*

She looked at the page.

It was a keeper's hand, as the Ferren one had been: round and careful, in ink gone brown. It was not the same hand. It leaned the other way, and it made its *s* long, in the old fashion.

*The 11th of the third month. Spring tide. At the low water in the evening a ring stood up in the basin at the outfall, a hand high, and the water in it would not go down with the rest. Lay down at the flood. Not in the fault column; nothing was at fault.*

*Took a fever after, the keeper. Two nights.*

*Dreamed there was no roof on the house nor on anything, and nothing over. The nothing was not empty and it was not dark. Something was attending. I write it here because my father told me to write what the needles did, and they did it too.*

*The refinery's lamps on the Long Quay went white about three in the morning and stayed lit. The low town's went out. They came on again of themselves at the half hour. Not grid.*

Mara read it twice.

She was aware of Hart in the doorway behind her, and of Yeo and Rusk by the window. She kept her face as she kept it over a fault report. It was harder than it had ever been.

*No roof. Nothing over. Not empty, not dark.*

She had sat at her own kitchen table in the fifth month of last year with her niece across the oilcloth, white and sweating, telling her what she had seen when she was thirteen and again on Classification Day. Mara had written it down in the girl's own words and sealed it and had the hour put on it, so that nobody could ever say that she had borrowed it. Then she had read a Ferren keeper's line in brown ink by a desk lamp, and had felt the floor go out from under her, and had said aloud that it was a comfort. *It isn't you. It's older than you. It's wider.*

And here it was again, two hundred miles from Ferren, in another keeper's hand, on the same night. *Something was attending.*

And a ring in the basin, a hand high, seventy-seven years before a ring four and a half feet high.

And the lamps.

She read the last line a third time. *The refinery's lamps on the Long Quay went white and stayed lit. The low town's went out.* The refinery on the Long Quay was Halden's. It had been Halden's since before the pump house was built. Every bank in it had been stepped since the patent, because that was what Halden's yards were. And on the night of the 11th of the third month in 2604, its lamps had gone white and stayed lit, and the low town's lamps had gone out.

A man could build a creed on that line, Mara thought. A sensible man. A man who had been alive that night and seen it with his own eyes could build his whole life on it, and be no fool.

"Mrs. Rowan," said Peverell.

"Yes."

"You've gone a colour."

"I've been up since four." Mara shut the book gently on its straw. "Keep that page, Mrs. Peverell. Under lock, if you've a lock. Don't let anybody copy it till I've written to a man at Ferren whose book says the same night."

"I've a lock." Peverell took the book back. She held it against her apron. "I'll say one thing, Mrs. Rowan, since you've gone a colour, and then I'll not say it again. I've kept this house nine years. My cells have not dropped a stroke in six months. Everybody in this town is proud of them, and so am I." She looked at Mara with her small eyes behind the spectacles. "I'll not be the keeper who stopped the pumps. If they're stopped, I'll be the keeper who wrote down that somebody else did, and why, in seven columns. I'd want that understood before you go any further."

"I understand it."

"I don't think you do yet," said Peverell. "But you will."

---

Mara went out onto the gallery alone, and stood at the rail, and took her left hand out of her pocket at last.

She did not lay it on anything. She held it in front of her and looked at it. It was a plain hand, with ink on the second finger and a scar on the thumb from a soldering iron. It had read the Ferren bank and then gone white to the wrist, and a healer had brought it back with a dram of somebody else's plain ash.

She would show the girl the page. That was the first thing, and it was not a choice; it was the girl's as much as hers, and more. She would show it to nobody else. Not Yeo, who was looking at her from the window, nor Rusk, who had written something in his black book that he had not shown anybody. Not the Board, and not the Order, and not the man at the Library press. Not yet.

And the hand. She looked at it and thought of a ring of grey with the bank's count shaking in the water inside it, and of thirty-six cells on two galleries, and of eleven minutes that the low town would bear on a making tide. She thought of twenty-one minutes of slack, which was all there was. She thought of what Hart had said: *as if the count goes out of the harbour and into that, and nowhere else.*

If that was so, then the harbour was still at the slack. All of it, except one thing.

And one thing was standing in it, keeping time.

She put the hand back in her pocket. She had not decided anything. But she had found the next question, and she knew what it was. That was more than she had known at ten to six.

---

On Wednesday at a quarter to six, Kiva went down the ladder to the grating with Hart.

Jude came too. He had asked at supper, very formally, whether he might, and Hart had looked at him over the table at the Works' lodging and said, *If you'll stand where I put you and not fall in.* Mara was on the gallery above with Peverell. She had shown Kiva nothing yet and said nothing, and she had a look on her face that Kiva knew from the year of the Ferren bank. It meant that she had something under the brass weight.

"Lay them on the bar," said Hart. "Light. The way you would."

Kiva knelt on the iron landing and laid the first two fingers of her left hand on the grating. It was cold and wet and furred with weed.

*It keeps time.* At once. Loud, louder than the bollard, louder than the pole in the creek. Four and four, the outfalls' gulps, the pumps behind the wall, the whole basin carrying it. She could feel it in her wrist.

"Seventy-four," said Jude, who had her other wrist. Pru had taught him that on the boat. "You're calm."

"Wait," said Hart.

He knelt beside her, with his right hand on the bars a foot from hers. His left held the watch. They waited. The tide was out. The mud lay grey, with a hand of water on it, and the dark place ten paces off.

At thirteen minutes past six, the grating went quiet.

It went quiet as the pole had gone quiet in Gannet Creek. Not *pushing.* Not *keeps time.* Nothing. She had her fingers on a grating a yard from six pump outfalls that were pouring water into the basin four and four, and the fingers said that nothing at all was keeping time.

"Quiet," said Hart beside her, very low.

And ten paces out, the ring came up out of the mud.

Kiva watched it rise. She did not breathe. She watched the grey rim come up, and the frost along its lip, and the mud sliding off. She watched the brown water stand up inside it, held, flat, four and a half feet above the mud. And she watched the water's surface tremble in rings, from the middle to the lip. Four times, and stop. Four times, and stop.

The outfalls gulped beside her. Four and four, in step.

And under her fingers there was nothing.

"It's not in the water," she said. She had not meant to speak. "It's not in the harbour. The count. It's gone out of the harbour, into that."

"That's what it looks like," said Hart. "I've never said it was so."

"It's still. The whole basin, at the slack. Everything's still." Kiva could not take her eyes off the ring. "Except that. All the count there is, it's in that. It's—" She stopped. She did not have a word for it. "It's taking it in. Holding it. The whole slack."

Hart said nothing for a long moment.

"Your aunt said the same thing yesterday," he said. "In nearly those words. On the gallery." He turned his watch an inch on his wrist. "I've been looking at it for six months and I never said it to anybody, because I couldn't prove it. You two have said it in two days." He did not sound pleased or displeased. He sounded like a man who had set down something heavy and was not sure yet whether he would be let pick it up again. "I'd want it in my book that somebody else said it first."

Jude was standing where he had been put, at the back of the landing, against the masonry. He was not looking at the ring. He was looking past it, at the sea gate, at the harbour, at the masts.

"Is that what it's for?" he said.

"What?"

"The slack. Twenty minutes, when it's all still, except that." Jude scratched his jaw. "I've been asking what ash is for since the autumn. Yeo says keeping the sea out. Maybe that's what the slack's for. So there's twenty minutes when you can see what's keeping time." He looked embarrassed. "I'd want it said I don't know anything about it."

"Nobody does," said Hart. "That's the first useful thing anybody's said about the slack in six months." He got up off his knees. "Write it down, Talley. In whatever you keep."

The ring stood for twenty minutes. At thirty-three past six the grating ached under Kiva's fingers, and the ring went down into the mud, and the held water came out over its lip in a white sheet and was gone.

Hart sighted at the staff with his level while it was still going.

"Four foot six," he said. "And a half. As yesterday."

---

Mara showed her the page at seven, in the keeper's room, with the door shut and Peverell gone out to her pumps.

Kiva read it standing, with her right hand on the table and her left in her coat.

She read *a ring stood up in the basin at the outfall, a hand high.* She read *took a fever after.* She read *no roof on the house nor on anything, and nothing over. The nothing was not empty and it was not dark. Something was attending.* She read it slowly, the way she had read a page in a reading room in the Compact Library with a sister of the Order at the end of the table.

She knew her lips were moving. She could feel them. She was counting, and she did not know what she was counting. She could not stop.

*The refinery's lamps on the Long Quay went white about three in the morning and stayed lit. The low town's went out.*

She stopped reading.

She was on a ferry in the middle of a brown river, with a hand on her wrist and a voice counting. There was no roof. There were roads, and lights on them, a great many, coming from every side, and some of them were bright and some of them were dull. Some of them were going out. Some of them had gone white. She had been lower than she had ever been, near enough to see that some had gone white. She had not told anybody. She had not told Mara.

She did not tell Mara now.

She stood at the table with her right hand flat on it and her lips shut, and she made them stay shut. It was harder than it had been on the ferry. On the ferry there had been nobody whose word it was. Here there was her aunt, a yard away, who had sealed a page so that nobody could say Kiva had borrowed, and who had shown her this one because it was hers.

It was not that she did not trust Mara. It was that she did not know what she had seen. She did not know whether *white* on a ferry and *white* in a keeper's book seventy-seven years ago were the same word. If she said it aloud, even here, even to Mara, then it would be a thing that had been said. And she had not decided anything.

"There's a line about the lamps," said Mara.

"I saw it."

Her aunt looked at her. Kiva looked back. Neither of them said anything for some time.

"You count when you read," said somebody in the doorway.

It was Hart. He had come up from the floor and was standing in the open door, with his right hand in his jacket. He was not looking at the book. He was looking at Kiva's mouth.

"I don't know that I do," said Kiva.

"I do it with a hand," said Hart. He did not come in. "I'll not ask what you were counting. I'll say that I saw it." He turned to go. "Peverell wants the room. There's a man from the Halden technical office at the door asking for you, Miss Rowan. He says he's been to Quiller's and the loft and the gate and the Ropewalk, and he'd like to sit down."

---

Mr. Adlam was sitting on a bollard on the outfall walk with his hat on his knee.

He got up when he saw her. He was as she remembered him, pleasant and fortyish, with a narrow gold cord at his cuff, but he looked tired, and his boots were grey with the dust of four streets.

"Miss Rowan. Forgive me. I've been chasing you round the town since breakfast like a bailiff." He put his hat on. "I've two things to say, and one of them isn't mine. I'll say that one first, because I was asked to."

"Yes."

"The Proprietor is in Saltmere," said Mr. Adlam. "He came down for the Measure, as he does. He's at our house on the Long Quay." He looked at her steadily, with real kindness. "He has asked to see the plate."

Kiva did not move.

"I told him it's held of the Line," said Mr. Adlam. "I told him what I know, which is that no hand but the bearer's is laid on it without a vote of the seven. He knew that already. He knows most things." He turned his hat brim in his fingers. "So we wired the Line's watch room on Saturday, under his name, asking the seven to vote. I'd like you to know that it was done properly and that I'd not have done it otherwise. The answer's expected by tomorrow's wire."

"And if they say yes?"

"Then it's yours to choose, I'm told. The Line's watch room was very firm about that when we asked how one asks. It said the watch witnesses and does not choose." Mr. Adlam smiled a little. "I'd have guessed it in any case. I'm afraid I read everything."

"What's the other thing?"

"Ah." He looked, for a moment, almost shy. "The other thing's mine. I've some old bench books. Our own. From the refinery here, and from the foundation's yard. Going back a long way." He hesitated. "I've found something in them that I don't understand, and I've an offer to make your four that you'll very likely refuse, and I'd like to make both in front of your captain and your book-keeper. Not on a pump-house walk." He looked past her at the basin, where the flood was coming in over the mud and there was no sign of anything having stood there. "Tomorrow, perhaps. After your crossing. I'm told it's at seven."

"Who told you?"

"Everybody in this town," said Mr. Adlam, "tells me everything. It's a great trial." He touched his hat. "Tomorrow, Miss Rowan."

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-22.md

# Chapter 22 — Kiva: Six of Six

Aske said the price at a quarter to seven on Thursday, at the foot of the gate mast, with his bag between his feet and the sea fog lying on the timber pond again like wet wool.

"The fifth went to a hand below the elbow," he said. "It sulked three nights. It was warm on Sunday at seven, and it's been warm every morning since, and I've seen it every morning." He did not look at Orla on her crate. He looked at Kiva. "I think this one goes to the elbow. I think four nights. And I think that if there's a seventh, whatever it's for and whenever it is, I'll want to have seen this hand warm on more than one morning before I let it go. That's not on the card yet. I'm saying it now so that it's said before and not after."

"Yes," said Kiva.

"That's the price, as near as I can say it." He picked up his bag. "Six of six."

There were more people at the mast than there had been a week ago. The Ropewalk had come again, not the whole school this time, but Carrow and Lyle and their two, and a few others in green. Dray was there on her stick, at the end of the Meridian row. The Clerk of the Measure was not. Noa had his tape threaded and Polglase had his, and Polglase had a book under his arm that Kiva had not seen him carry before. It was thick and black, with a cloth spine, and he put it on his reading desk beside the tape and laid his hand on it.

Perrin stepped up between the two desks.

"*Hand,*" he said. "*To Ember. Rowan, changing on three.*"

She went to the first cross.

It went as the other five had gone. She had stopped expecting it to go any other way, and she did not trust that, and she let it go as it went. The ward came up at her hip. Orla leaned from her crate and struck the iron leg of Noa's desk with her stick, once. The fold, from the far edge, to a penny. The shut. Nobody. The cold came into the hand, at once, a long way up. Then the Ember, glad. Six paces, low, through the fog, onto the second cross.

"One cliff," said Noa. "Twenty-six. Thirty in all."

"*Six,*" said Orla.

The hand was white. Kiva did not hold it up. She knew how far the white went by where the cold stopped, and the cold stopped at the elbow. It stopped in the joint itself. There was an ache there, deep, in the bone, as though the elbow had been knocked on a door frame a long time ago and had only now remembered it.

She put the hand in her coat against her ribs. The disk was cold.

Noa was bent over his tape. So was Polglase. This time Kiva did not go to the desks. She stood on the cross and waited.

"There's a notch," said Noa.

"Six seconds," said Polglase.

He had said it first. Noa looked up at him. The old man had his dividers out, a pair of brass ones that he must have brought in the black book's spine, and he had measured before Noa had.

"Six," said Noa, after a moment, measuring. "On every pen. Six seconds after the cliff. A week ago, seven."

"I said I'd looked," said Polglase. He opened the black book. "I went back through my book of times. Forty years of hairs on this mast. I'd never looked at them all together, Mr. Bexley. I only wrote down the minute." He turned the book on the desk so that Noa could see. "There's a hair at the springs, most springs, as far back as the book goes. One or two. Since the ninth month there's been a hair at every reading of every candidate at every spring, and some at the neaps." He took his spectacles off. "I didn't notice. I'd written it all down and I didn't notice. I said it was the harbour."

"Is it?" said Noa.

"I've no idea," said Polglase. "I'd like it written that I said so."

Noa wrote. Kiva watched him from the cross. She did not need to see the words. She knew he was writing *six seconds,* and *seven a week ago,* and *eleven in Meridian.* She knew he would not draw a line between them and anything, and that he would leave a space.

At the end of the Meridian row, Dray was looking at the mast. Her face did what it always did. But she had come forward a pace, without her stick, and was standing on the boards on both feet.

---

Orla signed at a quarter past seven, at Noa's desk, with Noa's pen, in the Chair's ledger that he carried in his satchel.

She did not say anything first. She took the ledger and read the line that Ysra Crest had written in the second month, in her level hand: *The fifth and sixth to be seen at the Saltmere gate mast, read by N. Bexley, her sponsor present, one in seven days and not faster. Not certified until the sixth.* Then she read the two lines that Noa had written under it, the fifth and the sixth, with their figures. Then she wrote.

*Rowan, K. The second crossing, hand to Ember. Seen six times on sanctioned masts, read by the office's reader: four at Meridian, two at the Saltmere gate. Certified, on my line, under her healer's card. O. Dane.*

She blotted it with her sleeve. She gave the ledger back to Noa.

"That's six," she said to Kiva. "It's on your certificate. It's in your name. I've only signed it."

"Thank you."

"Don't thank me. You did six. I sat on a crate." Orla took the green book out of her coat. She had had it since Sunday. She held it out. "Here. Your book. I read it."

Kiva took it.

She knew Orla had read the ferry. She knew Orla had read *I kept my mouth shut,* on the right, small. She waited for Orla to say something about it.

Orla did not. She looked at Kiva for a moment with her crooked face quite still, and then she looked at the mast, and then at her stick.

"It's a good book," she said. "It's got everything in it that ought to be in it. I'd say it's the best I've ever read, and I've read twenty years of candidates' books." She got up off the crate, in two stages. "Go and eat. Your healer wants that hand at noon."

Kiva went to the edge of the boards and stood by the black water with the book in her right hand and the cold hand in her coat.

It was certified. Two crossings now: Ember to still, from the summer, and hand to Ember, from this morning. They were in her name, on her certificate, on ordinary terms. They were one in seven days, read on a sanctioned mast, declared to a count, with the price said first. Anybody could read the terms. Anybody could hold her to them.

She had done three doors three times, and it had cost two fingers and an arm for four days, and nobody could hold her to it, because nobody knew what it was. It was not on any certificate. The Chair had written that it was not to be asked.

This was different. This was a thing with a page, and figures, and a sponsor's name and a reader's. It was small. It was a hand door folding a load and an Ember carrying it six paces. Pru could fold. Perrin could carry, in his way. A girl in the Ropewalk could probably do both at once, laid, and never feel the gap.

But it was hers, and it was written down, and she could do it again next week. She found that she was glad of it in a way that she had never been glad of the three.

She opened the book and wrote in the fourth column.

*Thursday the 10th. The sixth crossing, hand to Ember. Certified. Cold. As before.*

And on the left, under it: *A notch at six seconds. Every pen. Mr. Polglase says he has looked now.*

---

Aske had the hand at noon, in the loft, by the north window.

"Cold to the elbow," he said. He held two fingers in the crook of it for a full minute. "And it aches there. In the joint."

"Yes."

"You'd not have said, if I hadn't asked."

"I'd have said at four."

"At four I'd have known." He wrote. *Cold to the elbow at noon on Thursday. An ache in the joint, reported when asked.* "I'll see it at four, and tonight, and in the morning. I said four nights. I'll be glad to be wrong."

At four it was cold to the elbow still. At ten that night it was cold to a hand below. Aske wrote both, and said nothing about either.

---

Mr. Adlam came to the loft at half past eleven that morning, before the noon, as he had promised, with two books under his arm and his hat in his hand.

Seven was there: Perrin, Anwen and Jude at the long table under the south window, which Halden had left empty when it went to its lane. Pru was on the end of a bed with a cord in her lap. Kiva had come up from the gate. Noa had come with Mr. Adlam, because Mr. Adlam had asked him to. He stood by the stove with his satchel and looked uncomfortable.

"I'll show you first," said Mr. Adlam. "Then I'll ask. That's fairest."

He laid the two books on the table. They were bench books. Kiva had seen them in the Works: a bench book was the book that lived beside a bench where cells were made and tuned, and every cell that went across the bench was written in it, with its count. Nobody read them afterward. They went into a press when they were full.

The first was old, in boards gone brown, with a label on the spine in faded ink. *Saltmere Refinery. Bench Two. 2402–2431.* The second was new, in black cloth. *Saltmere Refinery. Bench Two. 2679–.*

"Our refinery here has stepped its banks since it was built," said Mr. Adlam. "Every cell that's gone into a stepped row on that bench has had its count written down when it was salted. *Fell in at so many to the minute.* It's a habit. Nobody needs it once the row's beating." He opened the old book at a page he had marked with a slip. "Here's 2402. A row of twelve, salted late with a pinch off the foundation's stock. *Fell in at forty-eight to the minute.*"

He turned to another slip.

"2431. Forty-eight." He laid the old book aside and opened the new one. "Last month. *Fell in at fifty-seven.*"

Nobody said anything.

"I went to the press at the refinery," said Mr. Adlam. "On Saturday, because I'd an hour. I took out every bench book there is. There are thirty-one of them. I wrote down one count from every year." He took a folded sheet from his coat and laid it on the table. "Forty-eight for a hundred and fifty years, near enough. Forty-nine about 2550. Fifty in 2604. Fifty-two by 2650. Fifty-five in the ninth month of last year. Fifty-seven now." He put his finger on the last figure. "It was slower once. It's quickening. And it's quickening faster now than it ever has."

"The Step," said Noa from the stove. "The beat. Four and four."

"The beat," said Mr. Adlam. "The patent doesn't give a count. It never needed to. A stepped row beats as one. Who cares how fast?" He looked round the table, and he looked, Kiva thought, genuinely astonished, as a man looks who has found that his own front door opens the other way. "I've been with the technical office for sixteen years. I've read the patent more times than any man alive, I should think. I had no idea. Nobody had any idea. Nobody looks at a bench book once the bench is built."

"Is it the cells?" said Perrin. "Getting quicker?"

"It can't be the cells. The cells are new every time; they're salted off the same stock. It isn't the stock; the stock's in a jar." Mr. Adlam sat down on the bench, rather suddenly. "It's whatever the stock keeps time *with.* And I don't know what that is. I thought I did. I thought it was the stock."

Noa had come away from the stove. He stood at the end of the table looking at the folded sheet.

"May I copy that?" he said.

"I brought it for you to copy." Mr. Adlam pushed it across. "I'd like it in your columns, Mr. Bexley, under my name. As before."

Noa took out his notebook. He did not open it at once. Kiva saw him look at it, the black notebook with three columns in it that he had carried since the spring: people, and rifts and spawn, and Saltmere. She saw him think about a fourth, and not draw it. Then he opened it and wrote the figures, in his narrow hand, at the foot of the third.

"The harbour bank," said Kiva. "The Regiment. What's its count?"

Mr. Adlam looked at her.

"I timed it on Saturday," he said. "At the pump house wall. Fifty-seven." He was quiet a moment. "It went into step at twenty past two on the seventeenth of the ninth month. Nobody salted it. Nothing was ever salted into it off any stock. And it's beating at exactly the count that my bench salted a row at last month." He shook his head. "I'd like somebody to tell me I'm wrong about that. Nobody has."

---

"That's the first thing," said Mr. Adlam. "The second's an offer. I'll make it plainly."

He put his hat on the table, crown down, and laid his hands on either side of it.

"You've two drams of plain ward-trace in a sealed tin. It combed silent on the seventh at your gate. Everybody in Saltmere knows; a clerk of the Board told a clerk of the Company, who told the whole quay." He looked at Jude, who had the book. "I'd like to buy it. Twelve drams of combed ward-trace, clean, at your gate, for the two. Six for one. That's a fair price this winter; it may be a low one by the summer. I'll not go higher, because I'd not want anybody to say Halden had bought plain ash off students at a price they couldn't refuse."

"What's it for?" said Jude.

It was his question. He had asked it of everybody. Mr. Adlam did not seem surprised to be asked it.

"For my comb," he said. "For my seventh reed. I've a reed that's cut to the Step, and every tin I hold it over says *trace* or *full* or worse. I've nothing left that's never heard a beat to hold it over and hear it say nothing. If I can't hear it say nothing, I don't know what it's hearing when it says something." He spread his hands. "I'd use a pinch a month. I'd keep the rest under seal. I'd write down every pinch. That's the truth of it."

Jude did not answer him. He opened the team's book on the table, at a page near the back, and turned it round so that Mr. Adlam could read it, and then he read it aloud anyway.

"*Sealed, medical, W, plain: two drams. Eight-tenths off a husk on the Sag in the fourth month, 2680. One and two-tenths given at the house of the Even Hand on Friday the 27th of the second month, to make good what eased M. Rowan's hand.*" He did not look up. "*For a hand. Not for a bank, a floor, a crossing or a sale.* That's under both lines. In my hand. Signed by our captain and our catcher. And in the Order's book, signed by a sister."

"Ah," said Mr. Adlam.

"Twelve drams," said Jude. "We've five held, at the moment. Five, near enough, for four boards. I'd want it said that twelve drams would change what we can do on every one of them." He shut the book. "And it's not ours to sell. It's for a hand."

"Then it's no," said Perrin. "I'm sorry, Mr. Adlam. It's under my name. It's no."

Kiva had not said anything. Mr. Adlam looked at her.

"No," she said. "It was given so that we'd not be asked this. The Master said we'd be offered a great deal. She said if it were ever spent on anything else, she'd want to be told." Kiva held his eyes. "I'd sooner not have to write and tell her."

Mr. Adlam sat for a moment with his hands on either side of his hat.

"I thought it would be no," he said. "I'd have thought rather less of you if it weren't." He took up the hat. "I'll find a pinch somewhere. There's a reference shelf in Meridian. Halloran may take pity on me, though I doubt it." He stood, and gathered up his two books. "Thank you for hearing me out. I'll not ask again. I'll ask somebody else, and I'll tell them you said no, and why, because it's a good answer."

He went to the stair. At the top he stopped.

"I'm told there's a wire for you at the Line's agent on the quay, Miss Rowan," he said. "It came at eleven. I didn't open it. I'm told it's from the seven."

---

The Line's agent in Saltmere was a ship's chandler with a brass plate and a telegraph instrument behind his counter. He gave Kiva the wire across the counter in a brown envelope with the Line's mark on it, and did not look at it while she read it.

*To K. Rowan, bearer, at Saltmere. Heard, at a sitting of the seven, the request of A. Halden to see the plate. The bearer may show it in her own hand, at a table, to whom she chooses, if she chooses. No hand but hers. Not weighed, not tested, not drawn, not copied. Four hands, three fists. For the seven, watch officer, Sag Line.*

She read it twice. Perrin read it over her shoulder.

"Four to three," he said. "That's close."

"It's enough." Kiva folded it. "It says *if she chooses.*"

"Do you?"

She thought about it, standing at a chandler's counter that smelled of tar and new rope, with her cold hand in her coat.

She had never shown it to anybody on purpose but once, in a room at Ferren with a canvas book on an oak table. It was the one thing anybody had trusted her with before they knew what she was. It did not do anything. It did not teach, or speak, or answer, or open a door. It had been warm once, after her first true crossing, and cold ever since, and she wrote that down after every crossing in the fourth column because a page said she must.

And a man who had been alive in 2604 had asked to see it. He had asked properly, through a Line that had voted. Three fists had said no and four hands had said she might choose.

She did not know what he wanted with it. She would not know unless she went.

"Yes," she said. "If he'll have my captain and my sponsor in the room."

"He'll have whoever you bring," said Perrin. "Adlam said so. On the stair, after." He took the wire from her and read it again. "He said, *Tell her the Proprietor expects nothing less.*"

---

Kellan Renn had never been in a room with the Proprietor, and on Thursday evening at seven he sat at the man's own table.

It was in the Counting House on the Long Quay. That was Halden's house in Saltmere, and had been since before the pump house was built: a tall narrow building of grey stone with a great many windows, all lit, and a brass plate by the door so old that the letters had worn to shadows. Inside, every room had lamps, and every lamp burned white and steady without a flicker. Kellan had counted nineteen of them between the door and the dining room. He had found himself counting them as Rowan counted things, without meaning to.

Halden License had won its two seeding lanes. It had won them well. Pettit had been pleased in his slow sleepy way, and Yarrow had been loudly pleased, and Hesketh had said nothing and gone for a walk on the Mole. And at five a man in Halden's livery had come to the loft with a card. *The Proprietor asks the Halden License four to dine at seven.*

So they sat at a long table in a long room with white lamps, the four of them, in their best black and gold. At the head of the table sat a man who had owned the Halden foundation and its pattern since before any of their grandparents had been born.

He did not look it. That was the first thing. Kellan had known that Glory stopped a body aging, as everybody knew, the way one knew that the sea was salt. He had not known what it would look like across a table. The Proprietor looked perhaps fifty-five. His hair was dark, going grey at the temples, cut close. His face was long and lean and pleasant, with deep lines from the nose to the corners of the mouth. He wore a plain dark coat with no gold on it at all.

His left hand lay on the table beside his plate, on a case.

The case was glass, with a brass frame, about the size of a small box of cigars. Inside it, on a bed of black velvet, there was a cell. It was an old cell, very old, of a pattern that Kellan had seen only in drawings: a squat iron drum the size of a man's fist, with a brass cap and a single brass handle, black with age. The Proprietor's left hand lay flat on the glass over it. It lay there through the soup and through the fish. When he lifted his wine glass, he lifted it with his right. When the meat came, a man in livery cut it for him without being asked, and he ate it with a fork in his right hand.

The hand on the glass did not move once.

"You're looking at my hand, Mr. Renn," said the Proprietor.

Kellan had been. He did not pretend otherwise.

"Yes, sir."

"Everybody does. I'd think the less of a blade who didn't." The Proprietor's voice was pleasant and rather dry, with no particular accent. It was the voice of a man who had talked to a great many people across a great many tables and had stopped performing for any of them. "That's the foundation cell. The first one that was ever stepped, in 2391, on a bench in a shed in a yard that's now under the Meridian tram depot. Everything Halden has ever salted, it salted from that. I keep its count."

"Keep it, sir?"

"Hold it. I've held it since I was a young man." He did not take the hand off the glass. "I eat with it there. I sleep with it there, on a table by the bed. I've not had that hand off this glass for longer than a minute in seventy years. I don't know what would happen if I did." He smiled slightly. "I've never been curious enough to find out. That's a failing in a man of science, Mr. Adlam tells me. He's quite right."

Pettit, beside Kellan, was very still. Yarrow had stopped eating. Hesketh was looking at the cell with her lips pressed together.

Kellan found that he could not look away from it either. He was aware of his own heart, which he was never aware of except in a lane. It was going slowly. It was going more slowly than it should have been, after a good dinner in a warm room. He thought of a pump house hall with six iron pumps going up and down together, and of the bills on the warehouse doors, and he moved his chair a few inches back from the table without knowing why.

"Ah," said the Proprietor. "You've felt it. Move as far as you like, Mr. Renn. Young people fall in with it. I've had clerks faint." He said it quite kindly. "The far end of the table's better. Nobody's ever fainted at the far end."

Kellan did not move further. He sat with his heart going slowly and his hands on the cloth and waited.

---

The Proprietor asked each of them about their lanes. He asked Pettit about his captaincy and listened to the answer. He asked Yarrow about the Ropewalk's lay and listened to that, and asked two questions that Yarrow could not answer and did not seem to mind. He asked Hesketh about her wall. Then he turned to Kellan.

"You stood for Team Seven last year," he said. "On their book. Mr. Adlam says you still do, for calls in Meridian."

"Yes, sir."

"And you stood against them in the second month, in a Marshal's lane, and wrote them down for your captain on four sheets of our yellow foolscap. And then told them, item by item, what you'd written." The Proprietor turned his wine glass with his right hand. "Mr. Pettit showed me. I thought it was the most honest piece of work I've seen from a blade in some years."

Kellan did not answer. He did not know how.

"I'd like to ask you about Miss Rowan," said the Proprietor.

There it was. Kellan had known since five, when the card came, that it would come to this.

"I'll answer what I can, sir."

"That's a careful answer."

"It's a careful subject." Kellan made himself look at the man and not at the cell. "I'll tell you what's on the record. Anybody could tell you that. She's seated in the Third Division, unclassified, and catcher on Seven. Her Draw read one-six on the new year. Her Hold reads Edge three-one, Ward four-five, Stance six-five. On one door the frame says Flame. On two it gives dashes. She has two crossings certified; the second was this morning, on that mast down the quay. She's read under the rule. One crossing in seven days. Her two fingers don't report." He drew a breath. "That's the sum. I'd expect nothing less of anybody who asked."

"And the three?"

"Three clean," said Kellan. "In all. A yard, the Pit, the Old Yard, last year. It's not on her certificate. The Chair's written that it's not to be asked of her." He found that his voice had gone level in the way that it went level in a lane, when he was carrying a thing carefully. "I know her count for two doors. I learned it on her floor, on her book, and I can't unlearn it. I'll not say it at this table, sir. I'd not say it at any table that wasn't hers."

The Proprietor looked at him for a long moment. His left hand lay on the glass. His face was quite pleasant.

"Would she do it," he said, "if she were asked?"

"It's written that she's not to be."

"By her Chair. I'm not her Chair." The Proprietor set his glass down. "I'm asking you, Mr. Renn, as a man who's stood in a lane with her, whether she would. If somebody asked her plainly, and named a price, and gave her the mast, and her healer, and every condition she liked. Whether she'd do it."

Kellan thought of a girl on a frozen yard with her arm gone white to above the elbow, telling Aske to say the price. He thought of a shed step in the second month and four people in a row with a ledger. *It's not for a floor.*

"No, sir," he said. "I don't think she would."

"Why?"

"Because it lives in one hand. And it cost her two fingers the first time, and an arm for four days the third, and her healer doesn't know it would come back a fourth." Kellan heard himself, and kept going. "And because she's spent the whole winter making something that three people can do instead. She'd say so. She'd say it to your face. I'd expect nothing else."

The table was very quiet. Pettit was looking at his plate. Yarrow had gone red.

The Proprietor did not seem offended. He seemed, if anything, pleased, as a man is pleased when a fact turns out to be the shape he had guessed.

"Thank you, Mr. Renn," he said. "That's the clearest account of her I've had from anybody, and I've had a good many." He turned to Pettit. "Mr. Pettit, your four is a credit to my house. I'd like you to know I think so. You stand on Monday, I'm told, at the Bench."

He talked to Pettit about the Bench for the rest of the meal. He did not mention Rowan again.

---

Kellan walked back along the Long Quay at half past nine with the others. The white lamps stood on their fluted posts without a flicker. Pettit walked beside him and said nothing for a long way.

"He's going to ask her," said Pettit at last.

"Yes."

"For the three. He asked you if she'd do it. He doesn't ask a thing like that over dinner unless he means to ask it again somewhere else." Pettit put his hands in his pockets. "I didn't know he did that. Ask for things. I thought he only owned them."

"He asked properly," said Kellan. "He asked the Line about the plate, and waited for a vote."

"I know he did. I'm not saying he's wicked." Pettit was quiet for a few paces. "He's the best man I ever sat at a table with. I think he's the cleverest. I think he's been right about more things than anybody alive." He sounded troubled, which Kellan had never heard him sound. "I'd not want him to ask me for anything I couldn't give."

They came to Quiller's. The loft's south window was dark; Old Four had gone to bed, by the sound. The north window had a candle in it.

"I'll be a minute," said Kellan.

Pettit looked at him, and at the candle, and nodded, and went up.

---

Kiva was sitting on her bed by the north window with her book on her knee and her left hand inside her coat. Aske was asleep in the bed by the door; Kellan had to step over his boots. Perrin was asleep with Coombe's rope for a pillow. Pru was awake. She looked at Kellan coming down the room and did not say anything, and then she turned over toward the wall, which in Pru was a kind of tact.

Kellan sat on the end of Jude's bed, across the narrow gap from Kiva's. Jude did not wake.

"He asked about you," he said. "At dinner."

"I thought he might."

"I told him what's on the record. The figures. The two crossings. The three clean, and that the Chair's written it's not to be asked." He kept his voice low. "I didn't tell him your count. I said I'd not, at any table but yours."

"Thank you."

"I'm not telling you so that you'll thank me." He looked at the candle. "He asked if you'd do it, if you were asked. Plainly, with a price, and a mast, and your healer, and every condition you liked."

Kiva did not move.

"I said I didn't think you would," said Kellan. "He asked why. I told him. I don't know if I'd any business to." He was quiet a moment. "He was pleased. As if I'd told him something he'd guessed. I don't know what he's going to ask you tomorrow. I don't know if it's the three. I think it is." He turned his head and looked at her. "I'm in his four. I wear his colours. I'll stand in a lane for his house on Thursday and I want to win it. I'd want you to know that I told you this anyway."

"I know you did."

"Don't go alone."

"I'm not going alone. Perrin's coming, and Orla." Kiva took her hand out of her coat, and looked at it. It was cold to a hand below the elbow, and the two fingers lay with the others. "Kellan. What's he like?"

Kellan thought about it honestly. He had always done that, and he had always done the sum aloud.

"He's the most courteous man I ever met," he said. "He listens to every word. He doesn't pretend about anything, not even his hand. He's got a cell under it that every Halden bank in the country was salted from, and he's held it for seventy years, and he says he's never been curious enough to let go." He paused. "And when you sit near him, your heart slows down. Mine did. It went to his count. He told me to move my chair."

"His count."

"Whatever that cell's keeping. He said young people fall in with it." Kellan stood up. "Sit at the far end of the table. He said nobody's ever fainted at the far end."

He went down the room, over Aske's boots, to the stair. At the top he turned round.

"He'll ask nicely," he said. "That's what I'd be careful of. Not that he'll ask. That he'll ask so nicely, and be so right about so many things, that you'll want to give him whatever it is just to have been asked by somebody like that." He hesitated. "I did, a bit. At the table. I'd want you to know that too."

He went down. Kiva sat with the book on her knee and her cold hand in her lap.

Across the room, Pru said, without turning over, "He's nice, Renn. I always thought he was a bit stuck up. He isn't. He's only careful."

"I know."

"I'll not tell anybody he came," said Pru, and went to sleep, or pretended to.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-23.md

# Chapter 23 — Kiva: The Proprietor

At seven on Friday morning the hand was cold to the middle of the forearm, and Aske wrote it down, and said, "That's two nights to go, if I'm right."

At nine Kiva was in the keeper's room at the pump house, watching her aunt write a page that could be found wrong.

Mara had asked her on Thursday night, by a note left on the loft stair. *Come at nine. I'd like you to carry a thing to the Clerk with me. M.* So she had come down through the low town in a thin rain, with the gratings rattling on the count, and found them all in the keeper's room with the door shut. Mara was at the table with a clean sheet of the pump house's fault paper and a pen. Hart stood at the window with his book of slacks open in his left hand. Peverell was at the press with the pump house seal, an iron thing like a nutcracker. Yeo was by the door. Rusk was in the corner on a stool with his thumbs in his waistcoat.

"It's a prediction," said Mara, without looking up. "Mr. Hart's and mine. It falls due on the morning of Wednesday the 23rd."

"What happens on the 23rd?"

"A spring tide. The next one. At dawn." Mara wrote a line, and stopped. "If we're right, the basin goes still at a minute that we've written down. The ring stands up at the outfall at the same minute, at a height we've written down. It lies down again when the slack ends. And while it stands, the water inside it keeps the bank's count, and nothing else in the basin keeps anything."

"And if you're wrong?"

"Then we're wrong, and it's under seal, and everybody can see exactly how." Mara dipped her pen. "That's the point of it."

Kiva understood. She had understood since the fifth month of last year, at a kitchen table, watching her aunt write down what she had been told about a dream and then take it to the Library to have the hour put on it. A page sealed before the thing happened was worth something. A page written after was worth nothing. That was all a seal was for.

Mara wrote. It took her a long time. She stopped at every line and looked at Hart, and Hart looked at his book and said a figure, and she wrote it. Twice they disagreed, quietly, about a minute. Once Peverell said from the press, "Not *the pumps.* The pumps aren't in it. *The basin,*" and Mara crossed out a word and wrote another.

At half past nine she put the pen down and turned the sheet round so that they could all read it.

*Saltmere harbour pump house. The 11th of the third month, 2681, at half past nine in the morning.*

*At the low water of the dawn on Wednesday the 23rd of this month, a spring tide, the slack in the basin will begin at nine minutes past six by the pump house clock, and not before seven past nor after twelve past. It will last not less than eighteen minutes nor more than twenty-two.*

*When it begins, the ring at the outfall will rise from the mud. It will stand at four feet eleven inches on the basin's staff, and not less than four feet ten. It will lie down when the slack ends.*

*While it stands, the water inside it will keep the bank's count, four and four, at fifty-seven to the minute or faster. Nothing else in the basin will keep any count, by the hand on the grating, though the pumps run.*

*We do not know why. We write it so that it can be found wrong.*

*J. Hart, fitter. M. Rowan, Grade Two.*

"Fifty-seven or faster," said Kiva.

"Mr. Adlam timed the bank on Saturday," said Mara. "He came to the door with his watch. He told me what his bench books say." She did not look at Kiva. "I'd not write a slower count than the one we have. If it's quicker by the 23rd, I'd want that written as well."

"*Witnessed,*" said Peverell. She came from the press and took up the pen and wrote at the foot, in her small crabbed hand. *Witnessed, Peverell, keeper, who does not know why either.* She gave the pen to Yeo. Yeo wrote *Yeo, officer* under it, in the brisk upright hand from the letter under the brass weight, and gave the pen back to Mara.

Rusk had not moved from his stool.

"Mr. Rusk," said Mara.

"I'll not sign it." Rusk opened his eyes. "It's not a Works document. It's a fitter's and a reader's. If the Works signed it, the Works would be saying it believed it, and the Works doesn't believe anything that hasn't happened." He looked at the sheet for a long moment. "I'll say I watched it written. You may put that."

Mara wrote it. *Watched written: Rusk, Examiner.* Then she folded the sheet in three, and Peverell put the pump house seal on it, and pressed it, and held it for a slow count of five.

"The Clerk keeps a press," said Mara. "For the Board. Sealed papers, with the hour on them." She stood up and put the folded sheet into an envelope and held it out to Kiva. "I'd like you to carry it. You know how a seal's carried."

Kiva took it in her right hand.

---

The Clerk of the Measure kept her press in a room at the back of the Board's tent, behind a canvas wall, in a black iron box with three locks.

She took the envelope from Kiva without a word, and looked at the seal, and looked at Mara. Then she wrote on the outside of it in a small precise hand. *Received at the Board's press at seven minutes past ten on the 11th of the third month, from M. Rowan, by the hand of K. Rowan. Not opened.* She put it in the box, and turned the three keys, one after another.

"When is it due?" she said.

"The 23rd," said Mara. "At dawn."

"Then it'll be opened on the 23rd at noon, in front of whoever you name and whoever I do." The Clerk folded her hands. "Is it about the harbour bank, Mrs. Rowan?"

"It's about the basin."

"I see." The Clerk looked at her for a long moment, with her shut face. "I'd like you to know that I've had three letters this week from the Harbour Committee, asking whether the Works intends to stop the Regiment during the Measure. I've told them the Board does not intend anything. I've told them the Measure is read on the floor of the Graving and not in the basin." She paused. "I'll tell them now that there's a paper in my press. I'll not tell them what it says."

"Thank you."

"I'm not doing you a favour, Mrs. Rowan. I'm keeping a press." The Clerk turned away. "Good morning."

Outside, on the boards of the timber pond, the rain had stopped. The gate mast stood black against a white sky. Mara walked to the edge of the boards and stood looking at the black water, and Kiva went and stood beside her.

"It's the eleventh," said Kiva.

"I know what day it is."

"Seventy-seven years."

"I read the date in the keeper's book. I read it in the Ferren one in the autumn." Mara had her left hand in her coat pocket. "I'd have chosen any other day to seal a paper, if the slack had let me. It didn't." She was quiet for a moment. "You're going to the Long Quay tonight."

"At eight. With Perrin and Orla."

"To show him the plate."

"If I choose. The seven said I may."

Mara did not say anything for some time. A gull came down on the black water of the timber pond and sat there, turning slowly.

"He was alive that night," said Mara. "He was in this town. The refinery on the Long Quay is his house." She did not look at Kiva. "I've read a line in a keeper's book about which lamps stayed lit. I expect you have too."

"Yes."

"Then I'll not say anything else about it." Mara took her hand out of her pocket and laid it, very briefly, on Kiva's arm, on the cold one, above the wrist. "Except that you needn't tell him anything at all. You needn't tell anybody. That's the only thing I know for certain about any of this. It's yours."

She went away across the boards toward the Works' lodging. Kiva stood by the water with the cold arm and watched the gull.

---

"If he asks for the three," said Kiva, "I'll say no."

She said it at six, in the loft, to Perrin and Orla. They were sitting at the long table under the south window, the three of them, with the light going and the stove not yet lit. Orla had come up the stair a step at a time with her stick, and had sat down at the table's end, and had said, *Well,* and nothing else.

"I know you will," said Perrin.

"I'm saying it so that it's said. Before, not after." Kiva had the Line's wire on the table in front of her, flattened. "I don't want either of you to say anything. If he asks. It's mine to say."

"It's under my name," said Perrin.

"The team is. The plate isn't. The three aren't." She looked at him. "I'd like you there. I'd like you to hear it. I don't want you to say it for me."

Perrin went red, and then he went the colour that he went when he was thinking very hard.

"All right," he said. "I'll sit there like a post. I've had practice."

Orla had not said anything. She sat at the end of the table with her stick across her knees and her crooked face quite still.

"I'll come," she said. "I'll sit where I'm put. I'll not open my mouth unless I'm asked a question to my face." She looked at the wire. "I've not been asked a question to my face by anybody in this town but one, and I answered it badly. I'd sooner not do it twice in a week."

Kiva looked at her.

Orla did not explain. She got up, in two stages, and stood leaning on her stick by the window, looking out at the harbour going grey.

"Eight o'clock," she said. "I'll meet you at the door. I'll not climb that stair again tonight."

---

The Counting House stood on the Long Quay between two warehouses, tall and narrow and grey, with every window lit.

Kiva had walked past it every day for eleven days and never looked at it properly. It was the kind of building that one did not look at: plain, old, respectable, with a brass plate by the door worn so smooth that the letters were shadows. But tonight every window was lit white and steady, all the way up, and the light came out onto the wet granite of the quay in squares, and none of the squares flickered.

Orla was at the door, as she had said. Perrin was beside Kiva with Coombe's rope on his shoulder. He had brushed his coat. Kiva had seen him do it, in the loft, at seven, with a clothes brush borrowed from Old Four, very slowly and with great concentration.

A man in Halden's livery opened the door before they knocked.

Inside there were lamps everywhere. Kellan had said nineteen between the door and the dining room. Kiva counted twenty-three between the door and the long room at the top of the stair. She did not mean to count them. She found she had. They burned white on brass brackets, and in glass globes on the newel posts, and in a great iron ring hung from the ceiling of the stairwell, and every one of them burned without a flicker.

And as she climbed the stair, her heart began to slow.

She felt it on the fourth step. She had been a little quick, from the quay and the cold and the knowing where she was going. On the fourth step her heart came down. It came down further on the tenth. By the top of the stair it was going slowly and evenly and heavily, like a pump.

She put her left hand on the banister rail. It was wood, old and polished. The first two fingers lay on it, lightly.

*It keeps time.*

At once, strongly. Four and four. It was not her own pulse. Her own pulse was in it, under it, going along with it, keeping step.

"Rowan," said Orla behind her, on the stair. She had stopped a step below. "You've gone white."

"I'm all right."

"You're not." Orla took her right wrist. She did not ask. She took it as she had taken a hundred wrists on a tear watch, and looked at the clock that stood at the stairhead in a tall case of dark wood, and counted. Kiva watched her lips move.

"Fifty-seven," said Orla.

She said it quite quietly. She did not let go.

"You've never been fifty-seven in your life. You're seventy on a quiet day." Orla looked up the last step at the door of the long room, which was open, with white light coming out of it. "It's him."

---

The long room ran the whole depth of the house, front to back, with windows at both ends. One end looked over the harbour and the other over the high town. There was a fire at one side. There was a table down the middle, long and dark and polished, with nothing on it but a white cloth at the far end, and two candlesticks, and a glass case with a brass frame.

A man sat at the head of the table with his left hand on the case.

He did not get up.

"Miss Rowan," he said. "Mr. Cade. Mrs. Dane." His voice was pleasant and dry and not loud. "Forgive me for sitting. It's not discourtesy. I can't rise without lifting my hand, and I don't lift it. Mr. Renn will have told you."

"He told me," said Kiva.

"Then he told you to sit at the far end." The Proprietor looked at her across the length of the table. She saw that he had seen her white face, and her hand on the door frame, and Orla's hand on her wrist. "Please do. I'd not have you faint in my house. You'd never forgive me, and I'd never forgive myself."

Kiva did not go to the far end.

She stood in the door and thought about it. The wire said *at a table.* It said *in her own hand.* If she sat at the far end, she would be thirty feet from him, and she would have to lay the plate on a table thirty feet away, and he would be looking at it down the length of a room as one looks at a ship from the shore. She had come to show it to him. She would show it properly or not at all.

She went halfway down the table and sat.

Her heart went slower. She felt it go. Fifty-seven, if Orla had it right; it felt like less. She felt her doors, too, under it. They did not lie down, as they had lain down in a small room with a fire and a woman standing at a window. They did the other thing. The Ember under her breastbone, which was always awake and always glad, came up. It came up as a dog comes to its feet when it hears a band in the street, and it wanted to go. It wanted to go along with the count, to fall in, to march. And the still door under everything, heavy and slow, wanted to beat. She had never in her life felt her still door want to do anything but hold.

She held them both shut. It was like holding two doors shut in a gale. She sat at the table with her hands in her lap and her teeth together and held them, and her heart went on slowly, slowly, in step with something.

"Further, I think," said the Proprietor. He said it quite gently. "One chair. There."

She moved one chair further down. Her heart came up. She felt it come up, a beat, two beats, as though somebody had let go of a rope. The Ember settled. The still door was still.

"Thank you," she said.

"Thank you for not going to the end," said the Proprietor. "I'd have understood it. I'd have been sorry." He looked at Orla and Perrin, standing in the door. "Please sit. Anywhere you like. Mr. Cade, you'll have the rope off your shoulder for a chair, I hope. It's Coombe's, isn't it? I'd know that knot anywhere. She tied one on my yard in 2660 and I've never got it out."

Perrin went very red and took the rope off his shoulder.

---

He was not what she had expected.

She did not know what she had expected. Something tall, perhaps, as she had expected Saltmere to be tall. Something like Thrale: enormous and quiet and very close. He was a man of about fifty-five by his face, lean, with dark hair grey at the temples and deep lines from his nose to the corners of his mouth. He wore a plain dark coat. His right hand lay on the table near a glass of water. His left lay on the glass case, flat, as a man lays his hand on a dog's head, and inside the case, on black velvet, sat an old iron cell with a brass cap.

There was nothing coming off him. She had stood near a Blaze and felt it from the rail like a stove. She had sat near a Glory master in a small room and felt nothing at all, and known that the nothing was enormous. This was different again. There was nothing to feel. There was only a count, slow and steady, in the table and the floor and the banister and the lamps. Everything in the room was keeping it, very quietly, as though it had been asked to and had found that it did not mind.

"You've read my face, I think," said the Proprietor. "You're the first person who's come into this room in a year and looked at me before they looked at the cell. Most people can't take their eyes off it."

"It's what you're holding."

"It is." He looked down at it. "The first that was ever stepped. On a bench in a shed in 2391, by a man whose name nobody remembers, because the foundation sealed the patent under a name that wasn't his. I keep its count. I've kept it since 2611, when the man who kept it before me died with his hand on this glass." He looked up again. "I don't know what would happen if I let go. I've told Mr. Renn that I've never been curious enough to find out. That isn't quite true. I'm very curious. I'm not brave enough."

He said it without any embarrassment at all, as he might have said the time.

"You asked to see the plate," said Kiva.

"I did. I asked the Line, and the Line voted, and I'm told three of the seven would have had me told no." He smiled slightly. "I'd like you to know that I'd have accepted that. I wanted to ask properly. I find I've asked improperly for too much in my life to start again at my age."

"The wire says I may show it, in my own hand, at a table, if I choose. Not weighed. Not tested. Not drawn or copied. No hand but mine."

"Then that's how I'll see it, if you choose."

Kiva put her right hand up to her collar and drew out the cord.

---

She laid the disk on the table in front of her, on the dark polished wood, with the cord still round her neck and her right hand on the cord. It lay there, dark and smooth, the width of her palm. In the white lamplight she could see the nine rings on it, very faint, going round, one inside another. There was still a thread of grey Kennel ash in three of the grooves.

The Proprietor looked at it.

He did not lean forward. He could not lean far; his hand was on the glass. He sat with his left hand on the cell and his right hand still on the table and looked at the disk across two chairs' distance. He looked at it for a long time. The fire ticked. The lamps did not flicker.

"Is it warm?" he said.

"No."

"It's never warm?"

"It was warm once. After my first true crossing, in the fifth month of last year. It's been cold since." Kiva kept her hand on the cord. "I write it down after every crossing. *Cold.* I wrote it yesterday."

"Near me, it's cold."

"It's cold."

"I thought it might be warm, near me." He said it quite simply. "I've thought so since the summer, when Mr. Adlam came back from the Library and told me what the Reader had said. A plate of nine rings, carried by the first teachers. I thought, if it's what they say, it'll know me. That's vanity." He looked up from the disk to her face. "I'm very glad to have been corrected. It's been a long time since anything corrected me that I couldn't argue with."

"I don't know what it is," said Kiva. "Nobody does. The first book's lost."

"I know. I'd hoped you'd know more than the book. I see you don't." He looked at it again. "Nine rings. I've a cell with one handle and a bench clerk's count. You've a plate with nine rings and a page that says *write down what it does.* I think we're both carrying things we don't understand, Miss Rowan, for people who are dead." He took his right hand off the table. "Put it away. Thank you. I'll not ask again."

She put it away. It went back under her collar, against her skin, cold.

---

"Seventy-seven years tonight," said the Proprietor.

He said it to the window at the harbour end of the room, where the Long Quay's lamps stood in a row along the black water.

"I was twenty-six. I was a bench clerk in this house, on the second floor, with a desk under that window. I'd been here four years and I'd written down nothing in my life but counts. *Fell in at fifty to the minute.*" He did not look round. "On the night of the eleventh of the third month, at about three in the morning, I was at that window. I couldn't sleep. I'd a fever on me. I'd had it since the evening."

Kiva did not move.

"Every lamp on the Long Quay went white," said the Proprietor. "Every one. They were ours. They were stepped, as everything in this house was stepped and still is. They went white, very bright, and stayed lit. And down there, below the wall—" he lifted his right hand an inch toward the low town, "—every lamp in the low town went out. All at once. As if somebody had put a hand over them. And they stayed out for half an hour, and then they came on again of themselves, and nobody ever found a fault."

He turned his head and looked at her.
