"I've thought about that for seventy-seven years," he said. "I've thought about it more than I've thought about anything. I've read every fault book in this country that has a line for that night. There aren't many. And what I've come to is this. Whatever happened that night, whatever it was that came, the things that kept time together went white. They stayed lit. And the things that kept their own time, each on its own count, the way the Works likes them, went out."

Kiva sat at the table with her hands in her lap.

She was on a ferry in the middle of a river, with no roof. There were roads, and lights on them, from every side, a great many. Some were bright. Some were dull. Some were going out. And some had gone white.

She had not told anybody. She had not told Mara, with the keeper's book open on the table between them. She did not know what she had seen. She did not know whether *white* on a ferry and *white* on the Long Quay seventy-seven years ago were the same word. And here was a man who had stood at a window with a fever on him, and seen lamps go white and lamps go out, and had built his whole life on it, and he was not a fool.

Her jaw ached. She found she had her teeth together so hard that the ache went up into her ears. Her hands in her lap were hot.

She kept her mouth shut.

"I'd step the country," said the Proprietor, "if they'd let me. Every bank. Every pump house, every lamp. Every box in every tenement. I'd have it all keeping one time, the way this house does, so that whatever comes next time finds it white and not out." He turned his eyes back to the window. "They won't let me. The Works won't, and the Board won't, and the patent says *not to be introduced to an unstepped bank under load,* because it's dangerous. It is dangerous. Your aunt knows what it costs better than I do." He was quiet for a moment. "And now your aunt is going to stop my best machine in this town. I'm told she's sealed a paper this morning. I hope she does it well. I hope she's right. I'd rather be wrong than have the low town flood."

"You think she's wrong."

"I think she may be right about the harbour and wrong about the country. I think I may be wrong about both." He looked at her again. "I'm a hundred and three years old, Miss Rowan. I've been wrong a great many times. I've never yet been wrong about what I saw from that window."

---

"I'd like to ask you something," said the Proprietor. "I'll ask it plainly, and I'll name a price, and you'll say what you say."

Kiva had known it was coming since Kellan sat on the end of Jude's bed in the dark. She had known it since Mr. Adlam stood on a pump-house walk with his hat in his hand. She found that knowing did not help in the least.

"I'd like you to do the three," he said. "Hand, Ember and still, in sequence, as you did them in the Pit and in the Old Yard. On a mast of mine, at the refinery, beside a stepped bank of twelve cells, with my reader and Mr. Bexley both, and your healer, and your sponsor, and every condition you name." He did not raise his voice. "I want to see whether my banks answer you. Whether the notch on Mr. Bexley's pens is on my needles too. Whether the lamps on my quay do anything. I want to know if what you are and what I hold are answering the same thing."

"It's not on my certificate," said Kiva.

"I know. Your Chair's written that it's not to be asked. I'm not your Chair." He laid his right hand flat on the table. "Forty drams to your team's book, combed clean at your gate. And for you, a seat on my roll whenever you want it, at a Torch's wage, under any healer's card you like, for as long as you like. It's not a bribe. I'd offer it to you if you said no. I'm offering it so that you'll know what I think the thing's worth."

Perrin had not moved. He sat at the end of the table like a post, with Coombe's rope in his lap, and he did not open his mouth. Kiva could see what it cost him. Forty drams. Their book had four and seventy hundredths in it, for four boards.

Orla sat by the fire with her stick across her knees and looked at nothing.

"No," said Kiva.

The Proprietor did not seem surprised.

"May I ask why?"

"Yes." She made herself look at him, and not at the cell, and not at her own hot hands. "Because it lives in one hand. Mine. The first time it cost me two fingers for good. The third time it cost me the arm to above the elbow for four days, and it came back to where it was, and my healer doesn't know that it'd come back a fourth time. Nor do I. It isn't a thing that I can do on a Friday and have back on a Saturday."

"I'd pay for that too."

"I know you would. That's the second reason." Kiva heard her voice go flat, and let it. "If it has a price tonight, it has one next week. And next week somebody will pay more, and somebody else after that. And then it's a thing that's bought, and I'm the only one who can sell it, and every time it's wanted somebody has to turn round and not look at me." She stopped. "I said that to my sponsor once. On a stair. About a method that only lived in my hand. I said I'd sooner it were a thing with handles."

"And this isn't."

"No. It's two gaps and nobody in them but me." She took a breath. "I've spent the whole winter making a thing three people can do instead. It's not done live yet. I'll not show it to you on a mast for a price either. But I've two crossings certified, in my own name, as of yesterday morning. You may read either one on your mast any week you like, one in seven days, with the price said first. That's what I've got that's mine to give."

"A single crossing tells me nothing that Mr. Bexley hasn't told the Board."

"Then I've nothing for you." She held his eyes. "I'm sorry."

The Proprietor sat for a long moment with his left hand on the glass.

"The country, Miss Rowan," he said. Not loudly. "Not me. Not Halden. If what I saw from that window comes again, the country will want to know what it's answering. You may be the only person alive who can show it."

"Then the country can ask." Kiva's hands were burning in her lap. "It can write it on a certificate. It can say who's to read it and what it costs and who pays. The Chair wrote that it's not to be asked of me. If the country wants to unwrite that, it can do it in the Chair's hand, in front of everybody. Not at a dinner table on the Long Quay."

The fire ticked. Somewhere in the house, a clock struck the half hour, and the lamps did not flicker.

Then the Proprietor did something she had not expected. He laughed. It was not a loud laugh. It was short and real, and it went across his lean face and stayed there for a moment.

"Thank you," he said. "I'd have asked once in any case. I had to. I'd not have forgiven myself if I hadn't." He looked at her with what seemed to be real pleasure. "I find I'm glad you said no. I didn't expect that. I've been asking people for things for eighty years and I can count on one hand the ones who've said no to my face and told me why."

"Was Kellan one?"

"Mr. Renn told me you'd say exactly that. Very nearly in those words. *She'd say it to your face.*" He turned his eyes to Orla. "Mrs. Dane. You've said nothing."

Orla looked up from the fire.

"I was asked not to," she said. "By her. I've done it." She paused. "She said no for herself. I'd want that known in this house. I've not said a word, and nor has her captain."

"I'd seen that," said the Proprietor. "I'd like you to know I'd seen it." He looked at Perrin. "Mr. Cade."

"Under my name as well," said Perrin. His voice cracked a little, and he did not seem to care. "The no. The team's book. I'd like it known."

"It's known," said the Proprietor. "I'll have it written down." He lifted his right hand from the table, a little, in something that was nearly a wave and nearly a blessing. "Go home, Miss Rowan. You're very hot. I can see it from here. I should have sent you to the far end and made you stay there, and I'm sorry I didn't."

---

The fever came on along the Long Quay.

She felt it at the first bollard. Her hands had been hot at the table; now her face was hot, and her neck, and the back of her knees. The cold wind off the harbour went straight through her coat and did not cool anything. By the third bollard she was shaking.

Orla stopped her under a lamp. The lamp burned white and did not flicker.

"Wrist," said Orla, and took it, and looked at her own watch, and counted.

"A hundred and four," she said. "And going up." She did not let go. "It's like the ferry."

"How do you know about the ferry?"

"It's in your book. *A hundred and twelve, by Finch.*" Orla's crooked face was quite close, and quite still. "I read your book every Sunday. I'm not a fool."

She did not say anything else. She took Kiva's arm on one side, and Perrin took it on the other, and they went along the Long Quay to Quiller's with Orla's stick ringing on the granite. Kiva walked between them. She did not think about anything except keeping her teeth together.

Aske was awake. He was always awake. He had her on the bed by the north window inside a minute, with the candle lit and his watch out and Pru on her knees on the other side.

"A hundred and eight," he said, after a minute. He put the back of his hand against her cheek, as her aunt used to. "Where were you?"

"The Counting House."

"With him."

"Yes."

Aske was quiet for a moment. He took the watch away from her wrist and looked at it in the candlelight, and then he wrote something on her card, standing, against the window frame.

"The ferry was a mile from the house of the Even Hand," he said. He did not look up from the card. "This is an hour from the Counting House. I'll not ask you anything. I'll write down what I've seen." He put the pen away. "*Fever, the second this year. Both near a Glory master, within the day.* That's all I'll write. It's all I know."

He sat on the upturned bucket that he had brought from somewhere and took her wrist again, and counted.

---

She lay on the bed by the north window with the quay lamps in a row along the black water, and she waited for the roof to go.

She knew it would. She had felt it coming in the long room with the fire ticking and her teeth together, as she had felt it on the ferry with her hands on the rail. She lay with her eyes open and her jaw shut and waited. The candle burned. Aske counted. Pru held her other wrist and said nothing. Somewhere at the far end of the loft, Old Four was asleep and Halden was asleep and Dray was asleep. Kellan was not; she could see his shape at the south window, sitting up.

The fever climbed. A hundred and ten, Aske said, at midnight. A hundred and twelve at one.

The roof did not go.

She lay and waited for it with every part of her that could wait. It did not come. There was the loft's ceiling, dark, with its beams, and the candle's light on it. There was the count, under the floor and the chandlery and the quay, four and four, going on. There was Aske's voice, and Pru's hand, and the fever going through her like a tide coming in over flats.

At about three in the morning, it broke. It broke in a long sweat that soaked the blanket through, as it had on the barge. Aske said, "There," and wrote it, and Pru let go of her wrist for the first time in five hours and sat back on her heels with her face in her hands.

"Nothing," said Kiva.

She had not meant to say it aloud. Her voice was very hoarse.

Aske looked at her over his spectacles in the candlelight.

"Nothing what?"

"Nothing came." She shut her eyes. "That's all. I'm all right."

He did not ask. He wrote, small, on the card. Kiva did not see what. Then he put the card in his bag and sat back on the bucket with his watch in his hand, as though he meant to sit there till morning, which he did.

Pru took her hand away from her face. She looked at Kiva for a long moment in the candlelight.

"I'll not ask either," she said. "I said I wouldn't. On the barge." She went scarlet, even in the dark. "I'd like you to know I want to, though. Very badly. I'm not asking."

"I know you're not."

"Good," said Pru. "Go to sleep."

Kiva did not sleep at once. She lay with her eyes shut and the sweat going cold on her. She thought of a man at a window seventy-seven years ago, with a fever on him, watching lamps go white. She thought that she had sat across a table from him tonight and he had told her that, and she had not told him anything. And she thought that she did not know what she would have told him if she had.

She would write it in the book in the morning. *Fever, after the Counting House. A hundred and twelve, by Aske. Broke at three.* On the left. And on the right, nothing. There was nothing on the right that she could write. Not yet.

She went to sleep at last with her left hand inside her shirt, on the disk, which was cold.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-24.md

# Chapter 24 — Kiva: Twelfth of Twenty-Four

"No," said Aske.

It was half past seven on Saturday morning. Kiva was sitting on the edge of her bed by the north window in a dry shift, with the soaked blanket gone down to Quiller's yard to be hung, and her lane boots in her hand. She had put the left one on. The right one was in her hand.

"Not the morning after a hundred and twelve," said Aske. He did not raise his voice. He never did. "Not on a floor eighty feet down a dry dock, against a four that's been waiting a fortnight for you. You'll sit on the tiers in a blanket and watch, and I'll sit beside you, and if you so much as stand up to see better I'll put you back down."

"It's a seeding lane. Seven needs a catcher."

"Seven has a catcher." Aske looked across the bed. "Finch."

Pru was sitting on her own bed with her cord in her lap. She had not slept either. She looked up when Aske said her name, and went scarlet, and did not look away.

"I'm the reserve," she said. "And the second catcher. It's on the entry. *Reserve: Finch, P., catcher.* The Board doesn't care which one of us stands, if the captain declares it." She looked at Perrin. "If the captain will."

Perrin was at the long table with the slate. He had been there since six, drawing pens on it and rubbing them out. He looked at Kiva, and at Aske, and at Pru.

"Rowan," he said. "Is it yours to say?"

Kiva sat with the right boot in her hand.

It was not hers to say. She knew that before he asked it. It was the captain's, and the healer's, and the healer had said it. She had sat in a fever all night with her teeth together, and her hands still shook a little when she held them out. She would not have let Pru stand on a floor with hands that shook. She knew it was not hers to say. She wanted to say it anyway, and she did not.

"No," she said. "It's yours."

"Then Finch catches," said Perrin. "Ward, declared. I'll say it at the gate." He put the slate down. "Finch. You've never stood a lane for us."

"I've stood them in the Old Yard against juniors every Tuesday since the first month. I've watched Rowan stand every one we've had." Pru wound the cord round her fist. "I'm not her. I don't call it as she does. I'll call it as I do." She looked at Kiva, quite straight, for once without going red at all. "I'd like you to watch. And tell me after what I didn't say aloud."

---

The Graving was full.

Kiva had stood at its landward lip on the first day and looked down eighty feet to a floor with men chalking lines on it. Now she sat on its tiers, two-thirds of the way down, wrapped in a grey blanket with Aske on one side and Orla on the other. Every tier above her and below her and across the dock was full. They were Saltmere green, mostly, and the brown and buff of the Tarn, and Meridian's colours in a knot near the middle. The iron and glass roof went over all of it. The light came down through the glass grey and even, as it comes into a church.

At the seaward end, three hundred paces off, the floating gate held the harbour out. Kiva could hear the sea on the other side of it. It was not loud. It was a long low breathing, coming and going against the timber. Under that, faint, through the stone of the tier she sat on, there was the town's count.

The pen was chalked on the dock floor, twenty paces wide, as at home. The Tarn's first four stood behind their hurdle in brown and buff. Kiva knew their captain. He was the broad slow boy with a face like a gatepost who had put his hand on a plaited girl's shoulder at the gate, a fortnight ago, over a stone jar that sang thin. The girl was on the reserve bench now, at the dock's side, with her plaits pinned up.

"Trewin," said Orla. "Their captain. Still door. He's very good. Slow, but he never moves when he doesn't mean to." She did not look at Kiva. "They sequence, the Tarn. As we do. They've got nobody who lays. They'll be a plain fight."

Seven came out onto the floor. Kiva watched them from above, very small. Perrin first, with the slate under his arm, and Anwen and Jude, and Pru at the end with her cord at her belt.

Perrin stood at the marshal's box and declared. His voice came up the dock's stone walls clear and flat, so that the whole Graving heard it.

"*Team Seven. Cade, stance. Pryce, ward. Talley, edge. Finch, ward, catcher. Rowan stands down, on her healer's card.*"

A sound went round the tiers. It was not the warm sound. It was a murmur of people asking each other a question.

Kiva sat in her blanket and let them ask it.

---

She had never watched Seven from above.

She had never watched Seven at all. She had always been in it, on the floor, at the hurdle or behind the litter, with her eyes on the ground and the heels and the far edge. Now she sat eighty feet up and fifty paces off. She saw the whole pen at once, chalk squares and posts and hurdles and eight people, like a game laid out on a table.

It looked quite different.

From the floor she had always seen what was close: a heel, a pole, a square of clean dirt. From here she saw shapes. The Tarn came over their hurdle at the bell in a square, two forward and two back, and the square moved down the pen toward the litter without any of its corners changing place. Seven came over in a line, Perrin forward, Jude and Anwen wide, Pru behind. The line bent round the litter like a hand closing.

The first carry went to the Tarn. Trewin planted at the litter's head before Perrin could get there. Their wall stood on the north side, their blade went south, and their carry walked the Commodore round the end of Anwen's wall as Halden had walked Gerald round the end of hers in the second month. It went over their hurdle two to two. Three. And their blade had touched Jude on the way, because Jude had gone for the pass and not for the blade.

"Four," said Orla, under her breath. "Talley went for the pass."

"He was told to watch the blade," said Kiva.

"Then somebody didn't tell him again." Orla's crooked face did not change. "Finch didn't. Watch her."

Kiva watched her.

Pru stood behind the hurdle with her ward shut and her cord at her belt. She was not watching the heels. That was the first thing Kiva saw. Kiva had always watched heels, because Pike watched heels, and Varley had learned to watch heels off her sheet. Pru was watching hands. She was watching Trewin's hands on the litter's poles, and the Tarn carry's hands. When the second carry began, and the Tarn's square came down the pen again, Pru said something, quite loud, that Kiva could hear even from the tiers.

"*Hands off! Left!*"

And the Tarn's carry took his left hand off the pole to reach for a pass. Anwen was there. She had been told *left,* and she had gone left, and her wall came up between the carry and the hurdle before he had finished reaching.

"*Down,*" said the marshal, far below. It was Jude, touching the carry on the shoulder as he turned.

"She watches hands," said Kiva.

"She's a hand door," said Aske, on her other side. "She folds with her hands. She'd watch hands." He did not take his eyes off the floor. "You're a still door. You watch where people put their feet."

Kiva had not thought of that. She sat with it.

The second carry went to Seven. They took the litter wide along the north side, as they had learned at the Ropewalk. They made the Tarn's square stretch to cover it, and the square was slow to stretch, because it had never been made to. Perrin tapped once and let his shoulders go, and Anwen took the poles. Pru called *now* to Jude, off the Tarn blade's hand coming down, and Jude was between him and the pass. The Commodore went over Seven's hurdle two to two.

"*Three to Seven,*" said the marshal. "And a down. Four to four."

The tiers made a sound. The Meridian knot in the middle stood up and shouted. Kiva did not stand up. Aske's hand was on her blanket at the knee, quite lightly, and she did not stand.

---

The third carry was the glass.

The Tarn had read the north side. Kiva saw Trewin read it from eighty feet up, as she had seen Carrow read the north wall, and she saw him say something to his carry in a low voice behind their hurdle. When the bell went, Trewin did not go to the litter. He went to the north side of the pen and planted there, between the posts and the litter, in a clean square of dock floor, and he did not move.

He was a post. He was not laid. He was one still door, planted, and he was the slowest thing in the pen, and nobody could go round him on the north side.

Seven went south.

They had to. The Tarn's wall was on the south side, and their blade, and it was a plain fight in the middle of the pen with the glass running. Pru called hands. Jude touched nobody, because nobody would come near enough. Anwen walked her wall from the far end and it came round, and the Tarn's wall did not come round, and for a long moment it looked as though the litter would go over Seven's hurdle again.

Then the glass ran.

The Commodore was lying on the middle line. Kiva could see it from the tiers, a canvas dummy in a tarred hat lying across the white chalk. The marshal came down off his box and looked. He walked round it twice.

"*The Tarn's half,*" he said. "By the head. A point. Five to the Tarn. Four to Seven."

Kiva let out a long breath.

Below, on the floor, Perrin stood with his hands on his knees. Pru stood behind the hurdle with her cord still at her belt and her face scarlet, and Kiva saw her look up at the tiers, at the place where Kiva sat. She was not looking for comfort. She was asking a question.

Kiva did not wave. She put her right hand up, flat, and held it there for a moment, the way a referee holds a hand up for a call that was right. Then she put it down again.

Pru looked at it. Then she turned round and went to shake hands with the Tarn's catcher.

---

"You watched hands," said Kiva, at the foot of the Graving's steps. "All three carries."

"I know I did." Pru was winding her cord. She had wound it four times since the floor. "I didn't know I would till I was standing there. I'd meant to watch heels, like you."

"Don't."

Pru looked up.

"You called *hands off, left* in the second carry," said Kiva. "Before their carry had moved. You saw it in his fingers. I'd never have seen it; I'd have been on his feet, and his feet didn't move till after." She heard herself, and made her voice level. "I'd not have got that down. Talley wouldn't have been there. I want you to know that I saw it. From up there. It's the thing you didn't say aloud. You watch hands because you are one."

Pru stood with the cord in her fist and her face going slowly from scarlet to something else.

"We lost," she said.

"By the head of a dummy on a chalk line."

"We still lost." Pru put the cord away. "I'm not grateful for it. That I stood. I'd want you to know that. It's not a favour. Aske said it and Cade said it and I'm the reserve and I stood." She looked at Kiva, very straight. "I'm glad you saw the hands. I'm going to go and be sick behind the Company's van now, I think."

She went. Kiva watched her go along the dock's edge, small and quick, with her sleeves rolled up, and thought that she had never seen anybody walk so exactly like a catcher.

---

The seeding board went up on the tent pole at the gate at six on Saturday evening, under a lamp, with the Board's seal on it.

There were twenty-four lines on it. There were a good many people round it. Kiva stood at the back with Perrin, and read it over the heads of a crowd in green.

*1. The Ropewalk School, Saltmere, first four. Won three.*

*2. Halden License, Meridian. Won three.*

*3. The Tarn, first four. Won three.*

She read down. Old Four was sixth. Team Twenty-Eight was ninth. The Ropewalk's second four was fifteenth. And at twelve, in the exact middle of the board, as plain as anything:

*12. Team Seven, Meridian. Won one, lost two. Points twelve for, sixteen against.*

"Twelfth," said Perrin.

"Of twenty-four."

"I can count." He did not sound unhappy. He sounded, if anything, rather surprised. "I'd like it known that I thought we'd be twentieth. After the Ropewalk. I lay awake on Wednesday thinking twentieth."

"We beat their second four."

"We beat their second four because you saw Treen. And we lost to the Tarn by a dummy's head because you were in a blanket with a fever." Perrin looked at the board. "That's twelfth. That's what we are. One lane won on a thing we learned the day before, and two lost to fours who were better or luckier, and our catcher down for one of them." He put his hands in his pockets. "It's honest. I'll take honest."

Jude had come up beside them. He had the book under his good arm, as always. He read the line twice.

"*Won one, lost two,*" he said. "I'll write that." He looked at Perrin. "And the other thing. *Beaten fairly.* I wrote it on Wednesday. You told me to."

"I know I did."

"I'd want it said I wrote it small," said Jude. "It took the whole margin."

---

"I want a second plant," said Perrin.

He said it on Sunday morning, at the long table in the loft, at eight. Kiva had slept from seven on Saturday evening until six on Sunday morning and woken hungry. Aske had looked at the hand at seven and said, *Cold to the wrist. One night to go, if I'm right.* She was eating bread and dripping at the table with her left hand in her lap.

"For the method," said Perrin. "The handed one. It's three people. Finch folds, Rowan carries, I plant." He had the slate in front of him, with three crosses drawn on it. "If my ribs go on the Bench tomorrow, or on the Carry, or on the Longshore, there's no plant. And then there's no method. Then it's a thing only three people in the country can do, and one of them's me, with sprung ribs." He looked up. "That's the thing Rowan said about the three. On a stair, once. That she'd sooner it were a thing with handles."

"There's nobody on Seven with a still door but you," said Anwen. "And Rowan, and she's the carry."

"Then we borrow one." Perrin put his finger on the third cross. "It's not live. It's a yard. I asked Bexley last night; he says nothing on Rowan's card touches it, because she lights nothing but her Ember, declared. A borrowed link, rehearsed, on a yard, so that somebody else knows the count."

"Who?" said Jude.

Perrin looked at the door at the head of the stair. Kiva turned round.

There was a broad slow boy standing at the top of it in brown and buff, with his hat in his hands and a face like a gatepost.

"Trewin," said Perrin. "He came up at seven and asked what *nobody* was. He'd heard Finch say it on the floor yesterday, in the third carry, to Talley. She said it once when she meant *nobody's got the blade.* He wanted to know if it was a call." Perrin went red. "I told him it was a call. I told him what for. He asked if he could learn it."

Trewin came down the room. He did not hurry. He stood at the end of the table and looked at Kiva, and nodded, slowly, as though nodding were a thing that had to be done properly.

"I'm a still door," he said. His voice was very deep and very polite. "I've been one since I was twelve. I've never planted on anybody's word but my own." He paused. "I watched your captain plant on the Ropewalk's boards on Tuesday, at his Hold's back, to be read. I'd like to know how you do the other thing. The pass. Where nobody stands together."

"It's not live," said Kiva. "It's not been done live. It's a yard thing."

"I know. I'd like to learn the yard thing." Trewin turned his hat round in his hands. "If it's wanted on the Longshore, I'd sooner have done it once on a yard than never."

Jude had opened the book.

"Four and twenty-eight hundredths," he said. He did not read the rest. "That's what we've got for four boards, and the Hunt pays shares. A go at the handed crossing is twelve hundredths, on our book. Finch's fold. Rowan's carry is her own thimble. Trewin's plant is his." He looked up. "If we do it, it's four and sixteen for four boards. If we don't, it's four and twenty-eight and one plant." He shut the book. "I'd do it. I'd want it said I thought about it."

"So would I," said Anwen.

Perrin looked at Kiva. Kiva looked at her left hand in her lap. It was cold to the wrist.

"It's my Ember," she said. "Not my hand. The hand's only the catch, and Finch has the catch." She looked across the room to the bed by the door. "Aske?"

Aske was sitting on the bed with his bag on his knees. He had been listening. He always was.

"One go," he said. "Your Ember, declared, single, on your own thimble. Not your hand. Not three goes. One." He stood up. "And I'm watching, and if you so much as sway after a fever like Friday's, I'll stop it with my own hand. I've never done that. I'd not like to start."

---

They did it in the Company's van yard at ten, behind the Long Quay, because it had a crane.

Rhea Sorn lent them the yard. She did not say anything about it. She sat on the tailboard of the Graywater van with Pike beside her and watched, with her hands in her pockets. Pike's hands lay on his knees. They were closed halfway, as they had been closed on Friday, no further.

The rig was the one from the Old Yard. Mace's line had slung a sandbag from the crane's hook on a cord, with a ring in the yard wall for the cord's other end, so that it pinned a second sandbag to the cobbles under it, the way a corner had pinned Selka Dray in a pit in the ninth month. The thing to be done was to take the cord off the pinned sandbag and carry it four paces east and plant it. Pru would fold it. Kiva would take it across. Trewin would plant it, a stranger, with a still door that had only ever opened on his own word.

"I'll call *nobody* twice," said Kiva to Trewin. "Once when Finch lets go, from the ground, before I've got it. Once from the air, before I've landed. You open on the end of the second one. Not when you see me land. On the word. You'll want to wait. Don't."

Trewin looked at her for a long time.

"I've never done anything on a word that wasn't mine," he said.

"I know. Nor had Perrin, the first time." She glanced at Perrin, who was standing by the wall looking at the cobbles. "He opened late at a count and a third, and the cord dropped. Then half. Then a quarter."

"How long did it take?"

"A week."

"I've got today," said Trewin. "And then I'm on the Bench."

"Then you'll be late," said Kiva. "That's all right. It's a yard. Being late on a yard's the point."

---

Pru folded at ten past ten.

She put her ward under the cord on its slack, low and flat, and tapped it once with her right hand, and folded it from the far edge to a penny. Kiva watched her hands. They were steady.

"*Nobody,*" said Kiva, short, with the swallow.

Pru let go. For an instant the load was nobody's, a penny of folded cord in the air above the cobbles, and Kiva took it. Her Ember came up glad under her breastbone. It was the only door she had open, declared, single, off her own thimble. She took the penny into it and went, four paces east, low.

"*Nobody,*" she said from the air.

She landed.

Trewin opened a count later. Kiva felt the penny begin to go. It did not go. It hung for a quarter count in nothing at all, belonging to nobody. Then his still door came up under it out of the cobbles, slow and heavy and very sure, as a post goes into wet ground. The cord was planted. Four paces east.

"Late," said Pike, from the tailboard.

It was the first thing he had said.

"A count," said Trewin. He was white. "I waited to see her land. I couldn't help it."

"Nobody can, the first time," said Pike. "Cade couldn't." He did not get down off the tailboard. He looked at Kiva across the yard. "And you called from the air before you were down. You always do. You call *nobody* when you're a foot off the ground, not when you're on it. You don't know you do it."

Kiva stood on the cobbles with her Ember shut.

She had not known. She had called it twenty times on a yard in Meridian and once on a floor in a pit, and she had never once thought about where her feet were when she said it.

"That's why it works," said Pike. "That's what you've got that Cade hasn't and this lad hasn't. You've been inside the gap. You know when you're out of it before your boots do." He nearly smiled. "That's the thing you didn't say aloud. I'm your second today. Somebody had to be."

Rhea, beside him, said nothing at all. But Kiva saw her take her hand out of her pocket and lay it flat on the tailboard between them, near Pike's, not touching.

They did not do it again. Aske had said one go. They had one go, a count late, with a stranger planting on a word that was not his own. Trewin went away down the Long Quay at half past ten with his hat in his hands, slowly, saying *nobody* under his breath.

Jude wrote it in the book. *Sunday the 13th. The handed crossing, on a yard, with a borrowed still door (Trewin, of the Tarn). Finch, 0.12. Late at the second gap by a count. Not live. Held, four and sixteen.*

---

Orla took the book at four, on her crate at the gate yard, with the mast standing up black behind her.

She read it with her lips shut, as she always did. She read Friday. *Fever, after the Counting House. A hundred and twelve, by Aske. Broke at three.* And the right-hand side of the page, which was empty.

She did not say anything about it. She turned to Saturday, and read the lane, and the line in Kiva's hand at the foot of it. *Finch watches hands. She's a hand door. I watch feet. Aske said so. I'd never thought of it.* Then Sunday. *Pike says I call from the air before I've landed. I didn't know.* She shut the book and gave it back.

"Twelfth," she said.

"Of twenty-four."

"I saw the board." Orla hooked her stick on the crate's edge. "It's what you are. It's honest. You'll go up or down from it on four boards, and nobody'll say you were given anything." She was quiet for a moment. Then she said, looking at the mast and not at Kiva, "I went to see him."

Kiva did not move.

"On Sunday last. At the pump house. On his landing." Orla's crooked face did not change at all. "It went badly. I said the wrong thing first and I couldn't find the right one after. He told me so." She took a breath. "I'd want you to hear it from me and not from him. That's all. It's not yours. It's mine."

"He said you'd come," said Kiva. "On the Friday. He said, *your sponsor's in town.* That was all."

"That's all he'd say." Orla almost laughed, and did not. "He's better at a shut door than I ever taught him to be. I didn't teach him that. Somebody else did. Or nobody." She got up off the crate, in two stages. "I'll not ask you what he said to you. Don't tell me."

"I wasn't going to."

"Good," said Orla. "I'd have thought the less of both of us."

She went away across the boards toward the officers' lodging, a step at a time, with her stick. Halfway she stopped and turned round.

"Six of six," she said. "I signed it. I'd want you to know that I've never signed anything I was gladder to."

---

Kiva went down to the pump house at a quarter to seven on Sunday evening, by herself, and stood on the outfall walk at the rail.

It was not a slack. It was the middle of a neap flood, and the brown water was coming in under the sea gate and filling the basin quietly, without hurry. The six outfalls poured into it on the count. There was nothing at the place ten paces out from the grating. There was only brown water, a little rough on the surface where the outfalls stirred it, and under it the mud. Under the mud there was a ring that would stand up again at dawn on the 23rd, at nine minutes past six, by the pump house clock, if two people and a sealed paper were right.

She took out her book. She did not open it to the page with the crossings, or the fever, or the lanes. She turned to the back, to a clean page, and she ruled it with her pencil into four columns, as she had seen them ruled in a small thick book with a cloth cover.

*Date. Hour. What I laid them on. Yes or no.*

She did not write *what.* She wrote *yes or no.*

Then she laid the first two fingers of her left hand on the iron rail. The hand was cold to the wrist still; the fingers were cold. It did not matter. They did not feel cold in any case. She laid them on the rail at a light touch and did not lean, and she waited.

*It keeps time.*

She wrote it. *Sunday the 13th of the third month. A quarter to seven in the evening. The rail of the outfall walk, Saltmere. Yes.*

That was all. It was one line. It told her nothing that she did not know. A thing kept time, in a town where everything kept time. She had known it since the first night, with two fingers on a bollard.

But tomorrow at a quarter to seven she would lay them on something again, wherever she was. And the day after, and the day after that. She would write *yes* or *no,* and never *what.* And after a year, if a man with a ruined hand and a book of slacks was right, the book would know something that the fingers never would.

She did not know what it would know. That was the point of it.

She shut the book. Behind her, on the gallery, a door opened. She heard a step she knew, slow and uneven, and did not turn round.

"Same hour every day," said Hart, from the gallery. "Never on purpose."

"Never on purpose."

"Then you've started." He did not come down. "I'll not ask what you wrote. It's your book." She heard him turn his watch on his wrist. "Your sponsor came. Sunday last. She'll have told you."

"Today."

"Good." He was quiet for a moment. "It went badly."

"She said."

"It went badly on both sides," said Hart. "I'd want you to know that I said so. To somebody." The door shut behind him. She heard his step going away along the gallery, slow and uneven, toward the pumps.

---

At seven on Monday morning, the 14th of the third month, Aske held the hand in the loft by the north window for two minutes by his watch.

The loft was full of people getting up. Halden License was dressing at the south end in black and gold, quickly and quietly, because Pettit's four stood at the Bench at nine. Old Four was arguing about breakfast. Dray was sitting on her bed with her left leg out straight and both feet on the floor, lacing a boot, and her stick was not anywhere that Kiva could see. Kellan was at the south window with his Halden coat over his arm. He looked down the room once, at the bed by the north window, and then away.

Aske let go.

"Warm," he said.

He wrote it standing, against the window frame. *Warm at seven on Monday the 14th. A sulk of four nights. Six of six; certified by her sponsor.* He looked at the line for a moment.

"Four nights," he said. "I said four. I'd have liked to be wrong." He capped his pen. "Whatever the seventh's for, and whenever, I'll say the price before it. It's at the elbow now, the reach. I'll want to see this hand warm more than one morning first, as I said on Thursday. Thursday's the soonest, by the card. That's what I'll write. You may tell your sponsor I said *soonest.*"

"She'll know what it means."

"She will." He put the card in his bag. "She's had a knee for twenty years."

He went down the stair for his breakfast. Kiva sat on the bed and opened the hand and shut it. All eight fingers that reported came in to the palm when she told them, and the two that did not came in with them, and lay there.

Perrin was at the foot of her bed. He had the coil of Coombe's rope on his shoulder and the slate under his arm, and his face was the colour it went before a floor.

"Nought," he said.

"I know."

"I know you know. I'm saying it." He looked out of the north window, at the harbour, at the Long Quay's lamps, which were still lit in the grey morning and did not flicker. "It's the fourteenth. The Bench is at nine. You're not at the Bench. It's on your card."

"It's on my card."

"Then you'll sit on the tiers with Aske, and watch Halden do it, and Old Four, and Twenty-Eight. And tomorrow there's the Carry." He went a deep dull red, all the way up into his hair. "That's mine. The Carry. I'd like it known that I've not slept."

"Nobody's slept," said Anwen, from the next bed, putting on her boots.

"I'd like it known all the same."

Jude was standing at the window with his good arm on the frame, looking at the sea. Pru was winding her cord. Kiva got up off the bed and put on her boots, both of them, and stood. She did not sway.

Out beyond the Mole, the sea was grey and breathing, every count at once. In the basin at the head of the harbour, under a hand of brown water and a yard of mud, there was a ring that had been seen by more people than one. In a black iron box with three locks at the back of the Board's tent there was a paper with a seal on it, and the hour, waiting for the 23rd. On a landing over a grating there was a man with a book of slacks who had said *it went badly on both sides,* and meant it. And down the Long Quay, in a tall grey house with every window lit, there was a man with his hand on a glass case who had asked her for the one thing that she would not sell, and been told no, and laughed.

Under all of it, through the floor and the chandlery and the granite of the quay, the town's count went on. Four and four. Fifty-seven to the minute, or faster.

The disk was cold. She had looked.

She went down the stair after the others, into the grey morning, to watch a Bench that she was not let stand at. On the first step she laid the first two fingers of her left hand on the banister rail, lightly, without meaning to, as one touches a thing for luck.

*It keeps time.*

It was not a quarter to seven. It was not on purpose. She did not write it down.

---

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.


---

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — STATE_LEDGER.md

# Kindling 2680, Book Two — state ledger

> Built by TAC

Status: **Book Two entry state only. No Book Two prose exists.** Everything in sections 1–9
is the accepted Book One exit state (evening of Sunday the 16th of the tenth month, 2680),
carried from `books/book-2680-opening/STATE_LEDGER.md`,
`books/book-2680-opening/provenance/MOVEMENT-006-CONTINUITY.md`, and chapters 47–48. Where
this file and the Book One manuscript disagree, the Book One manuscript governs. Section 10
records the checks made before this file was saved. Section 11 lists what was reconciled.
Movement blocks are appended below section 11 only from surviving Book Two prose.

## 1. Calendar

Every month has thirty days and twelve months make the year (Aske's "eleven weeks and four
days" from Monday the 10th of the tenth month to the new year confirms that no day stands
between the twelfth month and the first). A month therefore begins two weekdays after the
one before it.

| Month | 1st falls on | 30th falls on | Fixed points |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tenth, 2680 | Saturday | Sunday | Book One ends Sunday the 16th. First free day of Book Two: **Monday the 17th.** |
| Eleventh, 2680 | Monday | Tuesday | **The 1st:** Rhea Sorn's card goes back to her, signed or torn. Mara's hand is on a card "until the eleventh month." |
| Twelfth, 2680 | Wednesday | Thursday | Nothing fixed. |
| First, 2681 | **Friday** | Saturday | **The 1st:** Aske's ban on anything lit ends, if he signs the card up. The night van offer begins. Perrin joins his Hall's second team. |
| Second, 2681 | Sunday | Monday | Nothing fixed. |
| Third, 2681 | **Tuesday** | **Wednesday** | **The National Measure, at Saltmere.** Selka Dray "on her feet by the third month." Rhea Sorn's card ends on the 30th. The 11th (a Friday) is seventy-seven years to the day after the Ferren keeper's fever entry of 2604. |
| Fourth, 2681 | Thursday | Friday | Nothing fixed. |

Earlier anchors that prove the chain: the fifth month's 29th was a Wednesday (the Pit); the
sixth began on a Friday; the seventh on a Sunday; the ninth on a Thursday (the Regional, Friday
the 16th and Saturday the 17th); the tenth on a Saturday.

## 2. Kiva Rowan

- **Age and household.** Seventeen. Lives over Rowan Instruments with her aunt and guardian,
  Mara Rowan. A guardian's hand is still wanted beside her own wherever a rule says "under
  eighteen." No birthday is fixed in the supplied record; Book Two does not move her age
  unless the Book One manuscript gives a date.
- **Place.** *Rowan, K., Third Division, the Crooked Line: seated. No longer provisional.
  Catcher.* (the Chair, the 14th of the tenth month). Crooked Line third team; Team Seven.
  "Her sponsor's line and every condition on her certificate stand as written. She is not
  thereby classified." There is still no column for her; there is a page headed *Witnessed*
  with two lines on it.
- **The Home Line.** *Rowan, K. Entered on the Line's roll as catcher, autumn 2680. Hand as
  before.* Not a seat. No wage but a line-hand's day rate when called. Called by a Line
  foreman, with her sponsor's leave while she has a sponsor, "to set a mark, to read ground,
  to call a count that is hers and name the ones that are not." She has not yet been called.
- **Certificate, as it stands.**
  - Orla Dane's line: recovery only, dark hands; **and one crossing, Ember to still, low, to a
    mark, declared to a count, on a sanctioned mast read by the Chair's reader, one in seven
    days.** Not certified: any stack, any third door, anything Orla has not seen six times.
  - The Chair's conditions of the spring: one door declared to the referee before every
    sanctioned event, and any change declared; her book to her sponsor weekly; no live-cell
    events until her sponsor certifies in writing; one sanctioned-event incident ends her
    season. (The 2680 season is read. Whether that last line is re-issued for 2681 is the
    Chair's to say and is not on any page.)
  - The Chair's conditions of the 2nd of the seventh month: no second door on any Circuit
    floor except a crossing declared to a count and read at the time from a sanctioned mast
    by the office's reader; a stack so read is an incident; not at the Bench; recovery only on
    any floor with a contained rift. No crossing counts unless the tape shows the cliff. Noa
    Bexley is reader to the office.
  - **Selka Dray's mast rule** (any candidate with more than one door is read from a
    sanctioned mast by a reader of the Meridian office) has gone to Saltmere under seal.
- **Crossings on the record.** Ember to still: one under a field direction (the 29th of the
  fifth month), then Orla's six on sanctioned masts, and one begun and not landed in a lane
  at the Regional. Hand to Ember: landed once (the eighth month, 0.30). A crossing costs
  about 0.30 to 0.31 of a dram, of which about 0.26 is the gap. An unlanded crossing still
  spends the hand and the week's go. A stack is a miss, forbidden, and an incident if read.
- **Three doors.** Order: hand, Ember, still ("catch, carry, plant"); 0.59 whole. **Three
  clean in all: a yard (the 29th of the eighth month), the Pit (the 17th of the ninth), the
  Old Yard (the 5th of the tenth).** Uncertified, unclassified, and by the Chair's written
  entry "not to be asked of her." The last two were done under a recorded Home Line direction
  and an officer's hand, with the price said first by her healer; the last was offered and not
  asked. Never twice inside a fortnight. Aske does not know that the arm would come back a
  fourth time.
- **Readings.** Nothing has been measured since Classification Day, 2680: Draw 3.1; Hold,
  Edge 3.0, Ward 2.6, Stance 6.2; rank unsettled (the frame flickers); first door, none; the
  Call column dark. **No rank, Hold, Draw or bench figure has been re-measured.**
- **Body.**
  - **The first two fingers of the left hand have permanently lost fine sensation.** They
    bend, grip, and report pressure ("something's pushing"). They do not tell edge from flat,
    hot from cold, or one thread from two. Ward-trace ash and rest are ruled out as remedies.
  - The left arm lost feeling to a hand above the elbow on the 5th of the tenth month and came
    back over four days "to where it was, and not a hair less."
  - **Aske's card of the 10th: nothing lit until the new year. "Not a step. Not a ward for a
    tap." Dark hands and her voice.** His standing rule before that: one crossing in seven
    days, with the hand seen the next morning; the three-door is not on his card at all.
  - The left knee is still strapped and complained once, running. The three heat-lines run
    from the breastbone over the collarbone toward the left shoulder and upper arm.
- **Handoffs she names** (the list of the 30th of the ninth month): the cord, all three turns
  (Pru Finch); the slack of a hem on her left side (she takes a right-hand corner or is called
  to); tin lids with a lip; the pin of a seal and whether wax has gone off (Anwen has the
  wax); a button and a cuff; **the count of a thing that keeps time (somebody else's, named
  before).**
- **The disk.** Cold. It warmed once, on the 29th of the fifth month, after her first correct
  deliberate crossing, and has been cold through every crossing and all three three-door
  sequences since. Recognized by the Compact Library's Reader as the plate of nine rings
  carried by the first teachers and *released* by appointment. Held of the Line; borne by K.
  Rowan; not equipment, an instrument, or evidence; no hand but the bearer's without a vote of
  the watch; the bearer writes in the fourth column within the week after any crossing. What
  it is for is unknown. The first bearer's book is lost.
- **The dream.** No recurrence since Classification Day, 2680. No fever on any night of the
  autumn. She has Annick's phrase *the sky unroofed* and the Ferren keeper's line of 2604.
  **The name "the Unroofed Sky" has never appeared on the page.**
- **Her book.** Green cover, soft at the corners; a spring, a summer, and an autumn; no page
  ever torn out. It goes to Orla weekly and lies on the kitchen table nightly for Mara.

## 3. Team Seven

- **The four:** Kiva Rowan (catcher), Anwen Pryce (Hold), Perrin Cade (carry; calls *off*
  and keeps the brake), Kellan Renn (blade). **Jude Talley** stands as Kill by name, on the
  line for a head's share and not on the book. **Pru Finch** is a catcher with a sheet of her
  own and the standing reserve.
- **Standing.** Fourth at the Regional Measure. **Second step, in its own right;** the
  brackets came off on the 14th of the tenth month. It goes forward to the National Measure.
- **The book, signed by four on Saturday the 15th of the tenth month:**

| Line | Drams |
|---|---:|
| **Held** | **25.53** |
| **Sealed medical (W, plain ward-trace)** | **0.8** |
| By name to J. Talley, in one autumn | 7.8 |

  Check: 23.62 − 3.0 (second purse sheet and poles, for Pru) − 0.70 (call drams spent) − 0.59
  (three-door line) + 7.2 (to the book from Stopgate's pot) = 26.53; less 1.0 returned at par
  to the wardens of Stopgate = **25.53.** The loan of 4.0 to the casualty reserve went out and
  came back. Sealed: 2.0 − 1.2 (Mara Rowan's reading hand, entered as medical) = **0.8.**
- **The reserve cannot be made up.** There is no plain ward-trace to be bought in Meridian;
  every can has "had the hair" since the thaw. It "stays eight-tenths until somebody kills
  something quiet."
- **Not on the team's book:** Kiva's own weekly half dram; Pru's pay, which is off the
  Circuit's reserve card.
- **Red, and untouched:** Seven's own rung from the Pit, stepped, on Kiva's tag; Pru's bag
  from Stopgate on her own tag. Across the district 111 and 503.7 drams stand red-tagged from
  before, with 560.4 more from Stopgate. Where the red row goes is not said. The patent claim
  on the Weighbridge bag is before the Compact's court, unruled.
- **Saltmere.** **No warrant has been posted or entered.** Kellan reckons two drams a head at
  the least, five heads, ten drams. Nobody from the Circuit has been sent to the coast in
  eleven years.
- **Open inside the four:** which four Kellan stands with at Saltmere. He cut for the Halden
  license four at the Regional and declared for Seven under rule nineteen once the floor was
  struck. He counts himself among "five heads" on the 15th. No page settles it.

## 4. The offer

Rhea Sorn's card, slate blue, read in full to the four and signed on its back by all four on
Saturday the 15th of the tenth month:

> *Rowan, K. Catcher's second, to Pike. The night van, two nights in the month, from the first
> of the new year, when her healer's card is up. Dark hands. No door on my floor that is not
> on her certificate. Finch with her, for the cord, if Finch will. If T7 is called on a night
> of mine, she is T7's and I go short. Until the last of the third month. Then we look again,
> and either of us may say no. No seat is promised. R.S.*

- Kiva's two conditions, accepted: the *before* column is read out each night to all; her four
  have the first word.
- Pru Finch has said yes, on being asked by Kiva as well.
- A catcher's second "sets nothing. She calls nothing." She kneels at Pike's other hem and
  tells him afterward what he did that he did not say aloud. She is not to open her mouth
  until the bag is sealed.
- At most **six nights:** two in each of the first, second, and third months of 2681.
- The card is to go back to Rhea on **Monday the 1st of the eleventh month.**
- Rhea's slate has six columns: *Before. Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.*

## 5. Other people, as they stand

| Person | Body, place, and open thread |
|---|---|
| **Mara Rowan** | Grade Two, restored. Reader of the trunk for Examiner Rusk; has the hill, the bank, and the stopping of it. **Left (reading) hand starved to the wrist on the 6th and eased inside the hour: a week before she reads a drum, a month before she reads a bank.** The right hand has told her nothing since Linden Row (2677). Eleven sheets in seven columns, *How the Ferren bank was taken out of step, with what was got wrong first,* are tied and waiting on the dresser for the coast boat on Monday the 17th. Her page of the 29th (Kiva's dream as told) is sealed and timed in the Library's press. The Order's six questions are in her hands; two are to be answered. |
| **Orla Dane** | Accountable sponsor; her standing rides on Kiva's record. Holds **Jessamy Hart's card** and has shown it to nobody. Moved her crate a pace north. Bad left knee, stick. |
| **Anwen Pryce** | Has the wax and the sealed reserve. A relief's place on Doran Mace's ward line (the night van) for next summer; Mace left her name where it was (*Said so as she went*). Has told her mother. "Done minding." |
| **Perrin Cade** | **Two ribs sprung, not broken; strapped.** 380 kilos for thirty counts, cut in the stone. His Hall's second team from the new year. Minds, and says so. |
| **Kellan Renn** | Has the Halden yard, Tuesdays and Thursdays, nothing owed. On Seven's book "as a blade who's told where he's wanted." His mother signs "A. Renn, grader." |
| **Jude Talley** | Kill by name; 7.8 drams by name. Will stand at Saltmere, asked by name. Wants to see the sea. |
| **Pru Finch** | Her own sheet, her own red tag, the cord. Nipped to the first joint once. Goes to the night van with Kiva. |
| **Noa Bexley** | Reader to the Chair's office. His page reads *Not yet weighed* and *Not one thing.* Three columns before the Charter Board, Mr. Adlam second. |
| **Selka Dray** | Left leg broken above the knee and burned with cold on the 17th of the ninth month; in a chair; "on my feet by the third month." Unpersuaded, on the record twice. Means to beat Kiva in a lane at Saltmere, on a declared count, with a needle on her. Not a friend. |
| **Rhea Sorn** | Graywater. Wrote to the board; the brackets came off. Burned a card that said *mine.* |
| **Pike** | Graywater's catcher, six-and-twenty years. Will have Kiva as his second. |
| **Nairn** | Graywater's second blade; the leg healed; walking without the stick. |
| **Hollis Vane** | Home Line foreman ("the Sag"). Three directions to Kiva in his ledger, two with a cost column. |
| **Ysra Crest** | Placement Chair. Posted "seated." Keeps the *Witnessed* page. |
| **Healer Aske** | Keeps Kiva's card. Would not round the price down. |
| **Lowen Brask** | Old Four; named the hold and the paid-out string; two to the bar. |
| **Examiner Rusk, Mr. Adlam, Lumb, Ansty, A. Renn, Officer Yeo** | As in the Movement Six checkpoint. Yeo went south on the 8th and will ask for the Works' reader again in the spring. |

## 6. The grid and the rifts

- **The Ferren bank** (twenty-four cells, the Municipal Works') **is out of step.** The method:
  nothing in the water that keeps time; pumps stopped; every cell quenched on one word; fresh
  cans, each salted with a pinch of plain ash that "has never heard a beat"; a card with its
  own count under each handle, "not two alike in this room"; a reader between each cell and
  the next; each pump restarted on its own eight cells. It needs the main to be **still.**
- **Seven drums remain in step** on the Brickfields trunk: **19, 20, 21, 25, 26, 27, 28,** each
  alone on an open link, to be done one a day with the wardens' old winter stock. None has been
  done on the page.
- **Drum twenty-nine is fouled** (about thirty drams through a split plank). Its four streets
  are on boxes for about a month from the 6th of the tenth month.
- The Stopgate sluice works. The Crooked Line School's core house never fell in.
- **Saltmere's harbour bank, thirty-six cells, went into step at twenty past two on the 17th of
  the ninth month and has not come out.** Its pumps have not dropped a stroke. Nobody at
  Saltmere knows how to take a bank out.
- **Nobody has said** that water can step a cell, why a tear throws stepped ash, why husks
  stood a hold, or why the bank fell in on the 17th.
- The Home Line's suspension of held tears has not been lifted on the page.
- A can of civic stock in the Works' own store was cut with soot. Noticed; nobody has taken
  it up.
- **Evidence.** The notch on Kiva's pen falls eleven seconds after her second cliff (three
  occasions). The lamps' T-0 falls when a hold comes down. They are two things. T-0 entries
  on record: 2446, 2604, 2676, and three in 2680 (the Pit twice, Stopgate once). Noa's three
  columns (people; rifts and spawn; Saltmere) all rise, faster since the fifth month. One
  unvouched line says an overseas port's bank fell in at the same hour.

## 7. Knowledge boundaries

| Fact | Who has it | Who does not |
|---|---|---|
| **Kiva has dreamed, and what she saw** | Kiva; **Mara** (told in full; her page is sealed) | Everyone else |
| That a dream came on Classification Day, with no content | **Ysra Crest** | — |
| Annick's fever entry, read in the room | Kiva (the sixth month); Orla (2664). Neither has told the other what it says. Orla has not been told that Kiva dreams. | Mara has not read it |
| That Kiva's lips moved, counting, as she read Annick | The sister of the Even Hand; the Halden endowment's observer | — |
| The Ferren keeper's entry of 2604 | Kiva, Mara, Lumb (as a page in his book) | Aske, A. Renn, Lumb, and Mr. Adlam were out of earshot and **do not know that Kiva has dreamed** |
| The four T-0 entries, the 2446 margin, the disputed box, Orla's 2664 reading | Anwen, Kellan, Perrin, Pru, Noa | **None of them knows of the dream** |
| **Jessamy Hart's card and his request to read Kiva's work** | **Orla Dane only** | **Kiva.** Also Mara, the four, the Chair |
| That Hart is a pump-fitter on the south coast whose harbour bank stepped | Orla | Kiva |
| A color in the dream with no word for it | Kiva alone; on no page | Mara |
| What the bearer's book records of Mara's refusal in 2669 | Kiva, Vane | Mara, as far as the page shows |
| What the disk is for; the first book | Nobody | — |

## 8. Institutions

- The Regional result stands without brackets: **1. Halden License. 2. Old Four. 3. Team
  Twenty-Eight. 4. T7.** "The first company goes forward to the National Measure, at Saltmere,
  in the third month, 2681. Entries under each team's own hand."
- Circuit rules made in 2680 and still in force: a gate shall be left lit; any catcher may
  red-tag a bag on stated grounds; no ash above I-trace on any Circuit floor; every Measure
  dram combed at the gate.
- The Charter Board has three sheets under the Chair's seal. It has not answered.
- The Order of the Even Hand's offer of a year as a guest, one door, is "on the latch."
- The Crooked Line School's tenth edition restores the lane in seven columns, with Kiva's
  lines in the fourth and sixth.

## 9. Unresolved threads carried in

The cause of the Step. Why a hold stood. What the notch and the lamps are. Who else has
dreamed. The filter. The disk's purpose and first book. Kiva's classification. Whether three
doors can be made safe, taught, or done a fourth time. Saltmere's bank. The seven drums. The
red bags and the court claim. The cut can. The Charter Board. Hart. Dray. Rhea's "we look
again." Kellan's four. The warrant. The reserve that cannot be made up. Mara's sealed page.
The overseas line.

## 10. Checks made before saving (planning session, 2026-10-12)

| Item | Source checked | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Month chain | Checkpoint §1 and §9 item 21; ledger blocks for Movements Three to Six; Aske's count in chapter 47 | Consistent. The first month of 2681 begins on a Friday; the third on a Tuesday; the 30th of the third month is a Wednesday. |
| Certification | Ledger, Movement Five block; checkpoint §2; the board in chapter 48 | Seated, unclassified; one certified crossing (Ember to still); hand to Ember not certified; three-door not certified and "not to be asked." |
| Body limits | Chapter 47 (Aske, Monday the 10th); checkpoint §2 | Two fingers permanent; arm returned; nothing lit until the new year. |
| Team Seven's book | Checkpoint §6 table and check line; chapter 48 | Held 25.53; sealed 0.8; Talley 7.8. Arithmetic re-run and correct. |
| Rhea Sorn's offer | Chapter 48, card quoted in full | Quoted above verbatim. Six nights at most. No seat. |
| Hart's knowledge boundary | Chapter 48 (Orla's cutaway and the closing survey); checkpoint §0 and §4 | Private to Orla. Kiva has not been told. |
| National Measure timing | The board in chapter 48 | Saltmere, the third month, 2681. No day is given; Book Two sets the days. |

## 11. Reconciled for Book Two

1. **Hart's hand.** Orla says in chapter 8 that he "lost a hand" and left "with one hand."
   His card says "the hand, the bad one." Both stand: Orla never saw him afterward. He has one
   working hand and one ruined one that is still on his arm.
2. **Who dreams.** The series bible says that "a small number of high-rank practitioners"
   record the fragments. Book One's second account is a pump keeper's. The accepted book
   governs: the fever reaches ordinary people who sit beside banks and lamps; high-rank
   accounts are fuller. Book Two's plan builds on both.
3. **The notch.** The Movement Three block's "about forty seconds" was the keeper's guess and
   is superseded: eleven seconds.
4. **Kellan's "five heads."** Left open by Book One and settled inside Book Two (see
   `BOOK_MAP.md`).
5. **The seven drums** are 19, 20, 21, 25, 26, 27, and 28. Twenty-nine is fouled, not stepped.
